ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e389	At the request of the Control and Constitutional Committee, the President will propose to limit the exchange of lines to up to three lines with replies following submissions by the main speakers of each party group and parliamentary leaders and five lines with replies from members of the Government. – It shall be deemed to have been adopted.
ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e394	At its meeting on 5 May the Control Committee unanimously decided to establish a case on the role of the State in the transactions between Aker and Aker Solutions. In this context, the Committee has conducted consultations with the State Councils of Brustad and Andersen – current and former Minister of Industry. The background to the case is the transactions Aker Solutions AS had executed through the purchase of five shares from Aker ASA for approximately NOK 2 billion. Transactions included: Aker Oilfield Services – purchases of shares not already owned by Aker Solutions. The seller was, in addition to the Aker group, DOF Oilfield Services AS. The price of the shares was 878 million. kr. In addition, the repayment of shareholder loans amounted to 154 million. kr. Aker DOF Deepwater - purchase of Akers 50 pst. the share of the shares. The price of the shares was 189 million. kr. Repayment of shareholder loans amounted to SEK 76 million. kr. ODIM – purchase of 33 p.m. of the shares of Aker. The price of the shares was 513 million. kr. Midsund Bruk – purchase of 100 pst. of the shares of Aker. The price was SEK 88 million. Aker Clean Carbon – increase in ownership from 30 to 50 pst. in the case of a corrected issue. The price was 42 million. kr. As I said, the purchases have been priced at an estimated value of less than NOK 2 billion. The stock exchange announcement further showed that the companies in question had an investment programme in the coming years of nearly NOK 3 billion. In the exchange notification dated 2 April, it was stated that the financing of the transactions and the future investments would take place through a new bond loan of approximately EUR 15 million. Two billion dollars. Furthermore, it was said from the company: ‘Aker and Aker Solutions have shown extra care in the negotiations on the companies involved in the transactions. This has been done in line with good practice in related party transactions. A third company, DOF Subsea ASA, is also a party to the transactions.» The case attracted considerable attention when the transactions became known. The background for this was that it was speculated whether the transactions were particularly favourable for Aker ASA. Furthermore, it was claimed from many teams that the proceedings were deficient, that the case had not been dealt with correctly, that the valuation was too low, and that it had not been submitted to the General Assembly of Aker Solutions ASA. The state is, as you know, a significant owner of the Aker group. The background is that an agreement concluded on 22 June 2007 purchased 30 pst. of the shares in Aker Holding AS. The seller was Aker ASA. The measure approved the agreement by decision of 11 December 2007. Thus, the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Trade was authorised to complete the purchase, as stated. St. pp. No 88 for 2006-2007, Staten’s ownership of Aker Holding AS, and Innst. S. No 54 for 2007-2008. The State invested a total of NOK 4 926 917 001 in this transaction. According to the statutes of Aker Holding AS, the sole purpose of the company is to own shares in Aker Kværner ASA. Aker Kværner changed as a known name on 3 April 2008 to Aker Solutions ASA. Aker ASA owns 60 pst. of the shares in Aker Holding, the State of the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Trade owns 30 pst., Saab AB owns 7,5 pst. and Investor AB 2.5 pst. Both of them are part of the Wallenberg sphere. Aker Holding owns 41 pst. of the voting shares in Aker Solutions. The state’s ownership of Aker Solutions ASA is therefore indirect, through Aker Holding. The indirect ownership of the State thus constitutes 12 pst. of the shares in Aker Solutions. The purpose of the government to purchase into the then Aker Kværner was to secure the Norwegian owner's anchoring, to ensure that the head office function remained in Norway, as well as to help maintain Norwegian jobs. This is described as such in St.prp. No 88 for 2006-2007: ‘Aker Kværner ASA manages key future-oriented competence and is a central part of the petroleum industry. There are few owner settings in Norway other than the country that can only help ensure long-term ownership and Norwegian anchoring of Aker Kværner ASA. Agreements ensure the long-term and stability of the country, in cooperation with private industrial owners, on the ownership side for at least 10 years. This provides a good foundation for continued development and industrial exploitation of the company’s knowledge base based on Norway. The agreements provide a basis for Aker Kværner ASA to be developed and managed from Norway for at least 10 years in the future.’ From the proposition, it is also apparent that the principal owner of Aker ASA has made a declaration to maintain the majority ownership of Aker ASA for a period of 10 years. The ownership of Aker Holding is governed by a shareholder agreement between the four owners. The agreement was annexed to the landfill proposal. The agreement requires that the company's board of directors be composed of five members. Aker ASA shall appoint three members, including the Chairperson, the State appoints one member, and Saab and the Investor shall jointly appoint one member. The agreement contains specific provisions requiring the members of the board of Aker Holding AS to agree on certain types of decisions. The shareholders of Aker Holding are thus individually guaranteed negative control, or veto right, over certain decisions in Aker Solutions ASA. Proposals requiring a two-thirds majority, or more, of the Aker Kværner ASAs – now Aker Solutions ASA – General Assembly, shall be dealt with in the Management Board of Aker Holding AS. In the shareholder agreements it is agreed that in such cases, agreement is required between the chairman of the board and the members of the board appointed by the State and the Swedish group of ownership respectively. If this is not achieved, Aker Holding AS shall vote against the current proposal at the General Assembly of Aker Kværner ASA – now Aker Solutions ASA. This ensures that all shareholders of Aker Holding, including the State, have negative control over Aker Kværner’s statutes – that is, Aker Solutions. Similarly, it is agreed that transactions between related parties to Aker Kværner ASA – now Aker Solutions – pursuant to § 3-8 of the Shares Act as well as the pledge and sale of the shares in Aker require the agreement of both the Chairman of the Board and the members of the Board of Directors of the State and the Swedish Group of Owners respectively. Moreover, ordinary decisions are taken by a general majority. In St. prp. No 88 further states that Aker ASA will have a leading role and industrial leadership and control in Aker Holding AS. Furthermore, Aker ASA should facilitate the management of Aker Holding AS according to Aker ASA’s governance model. It is clarified that there will be no direct shareholder relationship between the State and the then Aker Kværner, and that the State will not affect daily operations. It is also said that the Ministry’s financial follow-up must be based on publicly available information from Aker Kværner ASA – now Aker Solutions ASA. So far on the background of the matter. After the exchange announcement on 2 April, there was considerable attention on the matter itself, not least by sending down the shares in Aker Solutions approximately. 15 p.m., and a hedge fund manager believed that the largest owner in Aker used Aker Solutions as a garbage box. Discussions focused mainly on four issues: The first is whether the transaction was properly processed and should have been dealt with at the general meeting. The second is whether the State, Saab and the Investor as minority owners had been adequately informed of the transactions or not, and who, if so, had been responsible for informing the State and the other owners of the pending transactions. The third is whether the transactions were properly priced or not, and thus whether and to what extent the State, Saab and the Investor as minority owners had incurred a significant loss, and last but not least whether the State as owner should have been more active and conscious at the time of the conclusion of an agreement with Aker ASA. Furthermore, there was considerable attention to the fact that the board member Heidi Petersen withdrew from the board of Aker Solutions on the basis of the Aker proceedings relating to the purchases, and that the state’s appointed board member, Berit Kjøll, also withdrew later. After the publication of the stock exchange notice on 2 April, a lot of column space has been used on what has happened, who has said what in which meetings both before and after 2 April. I am not going to go into detail about this, it is explained carefully in the vote and in the annexes, not least the Brustad Council letter to the Committee of 27 April, and also in the stenographic record that is attached. Let me just say that there have been many well-paid assignments for various law firms. The dispute has concerned the interpretation of the shareholder agreement, the treatment of minority shareholders, in other words shareholder democracy, and who is responsible for informing the major shareholders. In addition, the valuations of the companies have been good legal advice. Let me also state that agreement has now been reached between the different owners in Aker in an agreement of 11 May. It was agreed that the case should be addressed to the general meeting, which has just been completed, that the valuation is determined with the support of the Swiss investment bank UBS's valuation, and that it should be clarified in the shareholder agreement how future transactions should take place between related companies. The case has thus been clarified, with regard to the completed and future transactions. We are pleased with that from the majority. However, the case has received a political superstructure, which has resulted in a mistrust proposal from the opposition, and not least unusually strong and – let me also say – partly unreflected in the public debate. It has been said that this was so serious that it required national law. It's been compared to Russian oligarchs. There has been the biggest scandal under the red-green government, some have said, and some have called it political corruption. The representative Sponheim said that this was the most serious matter he had been away during his 16 years at Stortinget. Then the representative Sponheim must have slept well in the hour, because there have certainly been many things that have been significantly bigger and worse than this. The opposition has chosen to promote distrust of Brustad State Council. That's how far I'm able to remember, one of the thinnest mistrust proposals that's traveled here in the house. It doesn't have its reality in the Aker case itself, but it's a lost attempt to appear as if the opposition agreed on something. Let me say that it's not very often the opposition has agreed in the last four years here in the house. The attitude clearly shows that it is only the mistrust proposal that is agreed on, not the content of it. The Progress Party supported as a known country’s involvement in Aker in 2007 and considers that the State has been too active. On the right, the State considers that it has been too passive and has not supported the state's entry into the Aker in 20007. It shows that even on this issue, that is, on the content of the motion of censure, the opposition cracks in all directions. It shows with all possible clarity that there is a tactical assessment behind it, not the government's course of action. That doesn't mean that there's nothing we can learn from the Aker case. The first thing you can learn is that you have to guarantee the quality of the contracts better than the agreement that was concluded in 2007. It has been necessary to clarify the shareholder agreement with respect to related companies. It is easy to understand that this was not the central issue when the state entered Aker in 2007, but the experience has subsequently shown that this should have been done. In the future, especially if you choose this type of design – i.e. with a holding company, whose only task is to own subsidiaries – you will be sure that the board of the holding company is well informed about what is happening, not least the owners. Furthermore, I am pleased that the Department, at the Søberg Council of Ministers, informed at the open hearings that, in the future, it will ensure that representatives of the Electoral Committee are able to meet the wishes of the State, and thus have a greater influence on the choice of the Aker Solutions Board. There is a lesson that should also be used in other contexts, to ensure that the state has greater influence on the companies in which it is owned. The next thing you can learn is that you need to clarify how information flows between big companies and big owners. I note that there has been a divergent view of this between the board of directors and the group manager on the one hand and the minority shareholders on the other, whether information on the company's strategic or industrial arrangements has been satisfactory, both in terms of scope, content and not least who should be informed. If this is a real disagreement or a situational disagreement, I shall leave it unsaid, but for the future this should be settled in advance, not in retrospect. But there is a basis for believing that this is a situational disagreement when you know that Aker has used three legal assessments as a basis for not presenting the matter to the general meeting. That gives a clue as to where to find the answer. In the minutes of the owner’s meeting of Aker Holding on 13 March 2009, the Ministerial Council of Søberg stressed the need for ‘clean internal transactions, both in the context of Aker Solutions announced purchases of Aker Oilfield Services and in general’. However, in a letter to the committee of the group leader, Öyvind Eriksen, Aker ASA, it says: ‘We could hardly see the attention of the Council of Ministers as different from that of the Board of Directors – and not the owners – in Aker Holding to which we were to deal.’ This contrasts with the Department's clarification to the Committee that it is common for the Chairman or Executive Director to come and inform owners of matters of strategic interest or of importance to the company. I quote: ‘... the most common thing in the kind of case is that the Chairman and/or Group Manager of the Board of Directors comes to us’. This was expressed by the State Council of Brustad in the open hearing. The answer given by the Ministry of Industry and Trade to the Committee in letter of 3 June 2009 is the same. It says: ‘Share laws have no provisions specifically requiring the board and administration to communicate information to owners outside group relationships. However, it is common practice in Norwegian companies that companies themselves inform and involve the owners in important matters by the board of directors or the management of the day-to-day management.’;» It is made clear from the Council of State and the Department to the Committee that it is the responsibility of the company to inform and involve large owners in ownership matters, and, as the Council of State says, this is the thing to be sorted out, so that for the future we can count on this not to be a challenge. We are very pleased with this clarification and assume that the disunity and ambiguity that has been on these issues will be resolved for the future, either by explicitly clarifying the agreements you make, or by other appropriate means. At my discretion, of course, large owners are involved and included in decisions of a large industrial or strategic nature. I believe that all major owners in the Norwegian business community agree that this should be the case, and the country as a large owner should not, of course, be treated differently from other major owners who also require information before major strategic and industrial decisions are taken. The third thing you can gain experience in this matter is to emphasize how important it is that the state has an active and conscious ownership. There is an overall committee which considers the matter to be illustrative of the need to clarify the country's clear expectations as to the extent of company information of a strategic or industrial nature, how to inform the State, who should be informed, and not least the timing of such information. Furthermore, the Committee stresses the need for the active and clear participation of the State which owns, in line with other industrial owners, with clear, well-founded and independent positions relating to major strategic and industrial dispositions in the company, in other words an active and conscious ownership for the benefit of companies and for the benefit of society. What is particularly gratifying is that there is an overall committee that supports these comments, which shows that the State that owns must have an active and conscious position in the companies, which means that the State can no longer be regarded as a towing partner in another way, but that the State has a conscious and active relationship with its ownership. I am also very pleased that the state is a significant owner in Norwegian business. The fact that long-term, non-quarterly ownership gives companies the opportunity for long-term strategic industrial development is important for large companies, not least in turbulent times. That the state bought into Aker Kværner in its time was a correct decision. This case shows that ownership is constantly being challenged and requires an active and conscious state. In this way, the case of transactions between Aker and Aker Solutions has been a useful review of the country's involvement in business. Furthermore, it is not given – and that is the second part of it – that the country's involvement is of greater benefit to the state. You don't know that. It could be that the companies that have become partners here are good, industrial companies that have great opportunities in the future. Let me conclude by saying that the motion of censure promoted against the Brustad Council of State, in my opinion, is syllable and not substantively justified in this matter. I've explained that in detail. The opposition is, of course, in its full right to promote mistrust, and so do they. But it is with equal satisfaction I can say that we will reject the proposal from the position. State Council Brustad and the Government have done a good job in this matter.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e582	The case rapporteur has gone through the setting thoroughly, so I will not repeat much of it. Fortunately, it is rare that there is such a distance between the realities of a case and the language used, and the criticism that is raised, as it is in this case. I don't think there's ever been a motion of censure in this room that to those degrees lacks a factual reason, like this. Among the reasons for the mistrust proposal that Højres Per-Kristian Foss used when it was presented in the hiking hall was that the Council of State should conduct a media-driven ownership policy. The Council of State should have responded to the large-scale transactions between related companies in the Aker system only when this was criticised in the media. This production must at best be due to a shift in the memory of the representative Foss. After all, he must have completely forgotten the violent criticism of his own party trap, Right-wing nutritionist Torbjørn Hansen, presented at the entrance to and in what was to be the ‘silent week’ before the Easter feast. First, the representative, Hansen, used the term ‘candal’ and demanded a comprehensive, independent review. He then used the term ‘political corruption’ to describe the agreement that a broad majority here in Stortinget saw as an important move to ensure that Aker Kværner then remained a Norwegian-based and Norwegian-controlled company. The day after the attacks on the state council were further escalated. At that time ‘political corruption’ was not even sufficient, then what we must call the nuclear weapons of politics was brought up: the law of the Empire. What was the rationale? Well, the Aker Solutions exchange rate had fallen and the Council of State had allegedly wasted taxpayers' money. That the stock exchange price of another company in which the state is much heavier inside, Norsk Hydro, had fallen over the same period to a quarter of the top listing before the financial crisis, fortunately never has this kind of senseless accusations come about, as we also heard in the reply from Ingebrigt S. Sørfonn. This illustrates the main point. On the right, the criticism of the Aker transactions that had been raised in the media saw as an opportunity for rematch after the state entered and ensured that a traditional and important environment of competence with forward-looking growth opportunities would remain on Norwegian hands and Norwegian-based. When you don't get answers from the voters for your own policy, as the polls give clear evidence for a long time, it's a good right tradition to criticise the Workers' Party and the Government instead. And when the factual basis for criticism is weak, the use of words becomes more powerful and juicier. Right-wing criticism of this point also affects the Progress Party, which supported the state's entry into Aker Kværner. So this can't be a good contribution to the platform that the Right Hope is going to bring them back into the government offices after the election, like the Progress Party's little brother. While the Right threatened the State Council with national law, the Carl I. Hagen of the Progress Party believed that the Council of State should have respected the Easter stillness. For what was it he criticized the Council of State for? Well, she didn't dismiss the entire criticism that the state's ownership of these transactions was so small, about 60 million. kr, that if they were to show off, it was little to care about. So, in the opinion of the Progress Party, the Council of State was not to care about dealing with the violent criticism and accusations from the Right. Well, the Progress Party has become twice as big as the Right in the polls, but a State Council cannot allow itself to be so arrogant to a party as the Hagen representative here prescribes, even though the party is less than one's own. One might wonder how the Right would have reacted in the Easter week if the Council had done as Hagen recommended. Now, the federal weapon had already been developed, so the next one might have been a stand. You'd be sure to play the "Arrogance Card". If there is something that has been media-driven in this matter, it has been Right-wing behaviour, not the Council of State. At present, the reason for the mistrust proposal is that the Council of State showed a lack of involvement in the transactions. But in a separate comment from the Progress Party in the setting, it says: ‘In the opinion of these members, it would have been entirely possible, and perhaps most reasonable, by Brustad State Council to refrain from commenting when she became acquainted with the acquisition of shares and the statements made in the media.’ So what the Progress Party really thinks is that the Council of State should be completely passive. Nevertheless, the party supports a motion of censure on the grounds that the Council of State was not active enough. We are well beginning to get used to the fact that the Progress Party changes its position when they think it can be appropriate, but changing the position from one column to the next on the same page in a position in such a serious matter as a mistrust proposal, must almost be a new record. The government's reaction, and rightly so, was the deficient process leading up to the decisions of the Aker Holding Board on 2 April on five transactions and a bond loan between the Aker system related. The council never claimed that this could not be industrially reasonable or that the price was wrong. But the deficiencies in the process leading up to the decision were what was criticised, and for good reason. The lack of information about four of the transactions and the bond loan was not in line with the intentions of the cooperation agreement. It was also not in line with what is common between companies and large owners. Therefore, the other partner in Aker Holding, Saab and Investor also reacted. And the board member Heidi M. Petersen of Aker Solutions ASA went off in response to how this had been handled. So in addition, Aker Solutions' own audit committee has concluded that the transactions should be better anchored. I assume that the audit committee in Aker can hardly be said to act on behalf of the Government here. This clearly shows that the motion of censure has a purely political justification that is on the side of the Council of State's action in this matter. A motion of censure shall always be taken seriously. That is why, in this motion of censure, it is a signal to the outside world that unfortunately is also serious. Because by rolling out the second strongest gunshot the opposition is over, against the Council of State, there is an acquittal of the act of the largest owner in this company used in this case. What this opposition is saying is that as long as you're likely to be on the right side of a legal balance sheet on a sluggish line, it's all right to set aside the other shareholders in such extensive transactions between related parties. And then I'm not just thinking about the state and Saab/Investor, but the 60 pst. other owners of Aker Solutions. But even though the kiss gives a right to such a balance, I believe that ethics does not. It is not in line with good governance to act in this way towards the shareholder community. The political environment should also gather on the opposite signal, namely that shareholder democracy should be taken seriously. Small and large shareholders shall be treated equally. This is particularly the case when what happens may raise questions as to whether one or some of the shareholders has been assigned. Then the representative Foss will defend himself that our task is to control the Council of State and not Aker. But that doesn't mean that the violent criticism that the opposition is raising here because the Ministry of Economic Affairs didn't ask and dug if there could be anything more that they're not informed about, when they were close up to the board meeting, informed that there was not only one transaction – which was known for a long time – but two transactions, and stressed that these were to be handled in line with the shareholder agreement. This does not even qualify for a sin of omission. ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e862	In 2007, the Stortinget decided to purchase 30 pst with the votes of the Workers' Party, the Advance Party, the Socialist Left Party and the Centre. by Aker Holding. This was a newly created holding company, the only purpose of which was to own more than 40 pst. by Aker Solutions. The State and Aker ASA committed themselves to own the shares for at least 10 years. The rationale for the purchase, as set out in St. Prp. No 88 for 2006–2007 was to ensure competence and ownership in Norway from a long-term perspective and provide a good foundation for the development of the industrial competence of the company. The majority of the Economic and Social Committee wrote in the Opinion that ‘this case is a good example of the Government’s active industrial policy’. This acquisition was subject to strong criticism, both from independent professionals and from the opposition. The Christian People's Party is generally a member of state ownership in order to secure natural resources, head office functions and key competences in Norway. But it has to be done on neat terms. The Christian People's Party strongly opposed this purchase because it was inadequately justified, because the price was too high, and because it gave the state too little influence. The Christian People's Party was critical of the fact that the state did not use external advisors for quality assurance. We also thought that this could lead to unfortunate bonds when the state entered as the direct owner of the largest supplier company on a Norwegian base. The Christian People's Party also stressed during the deliberation that if the purchase was given a majority, it was essential that the state should not become a sleeping partner, but actively safeguard the values of the state to the extent possible. In April 2009, the news came that Aker Solutions made a larger share purchase in five companies from Aker ASA. The transaction was negatively received on the stock exchange, and the share price in Aker Solutions ASA decreased by 15 p.m. It was claimed that Aker Solutions paid overprices for the shares, and former hedge fund manager Kjell Erik Eilertsen believed in Today’s Business on April 3, 2009 that Smoke used Aker Solutions as a ‘cashbox’. This has also been mentioned by previous speakers. The Christian People’s Party considers that the agreement reached between Aker, Saab, Investor and the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Trade on 11 May 2009 does not involve substantive changes to the Decision of 1 April 2009, except that the case is to be dealt with at the General Assembly of Aker Solutions ASA and that the State has committed itself through the agreement to vote in favour of the transactions. We cannot see that the discussions that have taken place between the State and Aker since the decision of 1 April 2009 and the agreement of 11 May 2009 have brought about a substantial change in the decisions that the Brustad Government Council reacted so strongly to. The Christian People's Party also notes that the agreement of 11 May is in most respects contrary to the recommendations contained in Paretos' report, which was ordered by the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Trade after the publication of the transactions. In its letter to the Committee of 15 May 2009, the group leader, Øyvind Eriksen, refers to the meeting with the Ministry of 13 March 2009, where the Council of Ministers raised questions about the shareholder agreement. Eriksen states in his letter: ‘We could not easily perceive the attention of the Council of Ministers as different from that of the Board of Directors – and not the owners – in Aker Holding to which we were to deal’. This is in stark contrast to the Department's clarification to the Committee that it is common for the Chairman and/or Executive Director to come and inform owners of matters of strategic interest or of otherwise important importance for the company. There seems to have been a lack of clarity about reporting practices between the Ministry of Industry and Trade and the Aker leadership. The Christian People's Party cannot see that the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Trade has at any time made it clear to Aker that direct information is expected to be provided to the Department by the Chairperson and/or the Executive Director in addition to the information provided to the government representative. On questions during the hearing, and later by letter to the Committee of 3 June 2009, the Council of State could also not indicate that this has been clarified in the ownership notification. Nor can the Ministry of Industry and Trade demonstrate that the representative of the Governing Board appointed by the State had become aware of this need for a direct information channel between Aker and the Ministry in addition to the information she provided. The Christian People's Party believes that the primary responsibility for this ambiguity lies with the Ministry of Industry and Trade. The Christian People's Party assumes that for the future there is a better dialogue between owners prior to this type of transaction, and that there is agreement before and not after the transactions are advertised. We also expect that it will be clarified how and between whom the dialogue will take place, in advance. Through its representative on the Aker Holding Board, the matter became aware of planned internal transactions in Aker that would affect Aker Solutions ASA as early as 18 September 2008. On 24 February 2009, it became known that the management of Aker Solutions ASA had planned to buy up in Aker Oilfield Services AS. Furthermore, the majority of the planned transactions were briefed at the owner’s meeting on 13 March 2009. At this meeting the State was represented by four representatives of the Ministry of Industry and Trade. These did not object to the proceedings, nor did they ask for further information to the ministry. Meetings were also held between the Administrative Board representative appointed by the State and the Ministry of Industry and Trade on 25 March and between Aker Holding and the Ministry on 30 March. The Ministry has had every opportunity to obtain sufficient information about the transactions and procedures throughout this period, but has failed to do so. The Christian People's Party believes that the ministry had the opportunity to engage an independent financial advisor to assess the strategy and price of the transactions before the decision was taken. This could provide a basis for the country to assess both the price and the industrial strategy of buying-in on an independent basis before a decision was taken by the company. Instead, the Ministry acted passively both in terms of treatment and valuation. The Christian People's Party believes that this lack of commitment and activity on the part of the Council of State is highly critical and is not suitable for the interests of the state. The Council of State first showed activity and reaction after the public knowledge and partial criticism of the transactions. At that time, however, a decision was taken and an agreement was signed. The Ministry’s reaction was too late to allow the decision on the transactions to be overturned. Thus, decisions were taken without the Ministry having made an independent assessment of the transactions, as has subsequently been seen to be necessary. The Christian People's Party appreciates the broad political agreement reached during the examination of the case that the Aker case underlines the need for active and conscious ownership by the state, in line with the announcement of ownership, and it is duly underlined also by the case-rapporteur Øystein Djupedal earlier in the debate. The Christian People's Party has noted in particular that an overall committee, including the representatives of the Government Parties, believes that: «the case illustrates the need for clarification of the country's clear expectations as to the extent of company information of a strategic or industrial nature, how to inform the State, who is obliged to inform, and the time of such information». Furthermore, the Committee states that it will: ‘emphasise the need for the active and clear participation of the State of ownership, in line with other industrial owners, with clear, well-founded and independent positions relating to major strategic industrial dispositions in the company’. The Christian People's Party takes this as a clear signal that an overall control and constitution committee, and not the government parties, are satisfied with the way the government has managed ownership in Aker Holding. The behaviour of the Council of State in safeguarding the state's ownership of Aker Holding, based on the reasons I have stated here, has not been suitable for the interests of the state, and is highly critical. The Christian People's Party therefore regrets the handling of the case by the Council of State. The government's parties are trying to make a mockery of the mistrust proposal that is being promoted in the setting – and I think it is also here in the Chamber today. Mistletoe is something most of the parties in the Stortinge don't promote in vain, I think we're aware of. As far as I have brought about the clean, no such proposal has been made since the last Stoltenberg government in 2000. And it was a proposal from only one party. Mistylit is serious. When the Christian People's Party promotes it in concrete matters, a government council has committed acts that are highly critical, or has failed to do anything, which also has serious consequences. In this case, as I have described in my post, what has happened. I expect the council here in the Chamber to show a completely different attitude and take self-criticism, contrary to what has been the case so far in this matter.
ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e930	This case started two years ago, when the State secured decisive influence over one of Norway's largest and most important companies through acquisitions of over 12 p.m. of the shares. Former Aker Kværner, now Aker Solutions ASA, represents a national competence environment within one of Norway’s most important industries. The agreement was widely agreed in Stortinget. All parties, except the Right, Christian People's Party and the Left, voted in favour of the purchase. In winter, several intra-Aker transactions were carried out, from Aker ASA to Aker Solutions AS, a wholly-owned subsidiary of Aker Solutions ASA. The transactions concerned those companies. It was Aker Oilfield Services for approx. NOK 1 billion, Aker DOF Deepwater to SEK 250 million. kr, ODIM at NOK 0.5 billion, Midsund Use at just under 100 million. kr, and finally an increased ownership interest in Aker Clean Carbon to just under 50 million. kr. Overall, these purchases thus had a value of about 2 billion – that is, significant amounts and a significant amount whether we measure in money or in activity. These transactions were carried out without any prior information or consultation of the State that owns them by the company or by the board of directors, and therefore had no opportunity to assess the consequences of the transaction. Therefore, when the case became known through the exchange notification on 2 April, one of the company's major owners was unknown as well as the industrial thinking behind it. At that time there had been no direct contact between either the Executive Director or the Administrative Board and the Ministry where full information on the transactions was provided. The opposition believes that this is the fault and responsibility of the Council of State, a position I think it is difficult to find coverage for. There had been contacts between the government at the department and the company several times during the winter, without full information about the transactions being provided. 13 March is an important date in this context. Then there was a meeting between the owners of Aker Holding. A general briefing was provided on the company’s strategy, activities and organisation. At the end of the meeting some ownership questions were raised. It was briefed that it was planned to purchase Aker Oilfield Services, an information that was not new, since this was announced in the company's quarter-quarter report on 24 February. There was no information about the price, progress or other conditions, and Interestingly, no mention was made of the other transactions, according to the Ministry. On the other hand, the Ministry stressed the importance of the clean-up of this transaction in particular and in other related transactions in general. Furthermore, Aker Solutions ASA’s responsibility to ensure that there was no criticism of the transactions could be addressed, and the importance that valuations could be documented was underlined. On 27 March, there is a meeting between Berit Kjøll and the Ministry before the General Assembly on the subject of the administrative work of the company in general. Here, the transactions in Aker Oilfield Services became a new topic. Kjøll believed that trade was industrially correct and that it would be carried out in the right way – in the light of brief, oral briefings of the Governing Councils on 18 September 2008 and 24 February 2009. Prior to the meeting, Kjøll requested more information from the Executive Director in order to better inform the Department. This was given in the form of an e-mail with an attachment of 27 pages, where the four other transactions were mentioned. This was completely new information for Kjøll at the time. For this reason, and because of the accompanying message that she was on the company's inside list, this information was not passed on to the ministry. On March 30, the ordinary general meeting in Aker Holding was on ordinary matters. Here, the Chairman of the Board of Directors asked Kjøll to take up the transactions that were to be dealt with at the board meeting later in the day. Manager Øyvind Eriksen briefed on parts of the company's strategy in addition to the already known transaction with Aker Oilfield Services. Then it was mentioned the purchase of shares in Odin and a directed issue against Aker Clean Carbon. No other information was provided about the transactions. The Ministry replied that information is not normally received on an ongoing basis, but that it is expected that the company itself will contact when information relevant to the owner is available. It was further underlined that it is the company’s responsibility to contact the owner when they consider that the need for information arises. This was not a prepared meeting. It was carried out on the initiative of Kjøll, and it was only an hour before the Governing Board meeting. At the board meeting in Aker Holding on 30 March, the transactions were briefed. The Swedish owners were to be briefed on the transactions, where Kjøll immediately asked to participate. The orientation was carried out, well to notice without the cold being invited to attend. On April 1, Kjøll then contacted the department and briefed per. telephone about the transactions to be executed, in the light of the information given at the Governing Board meeting on 30 March. On 2 April, Aker Solutions ASA and AS sent out an exchange message announcing the full volume of the four transactions and the issue, in addition, that this was intended to be funded by a larger bond loan with Aker ASA as the largest lender. It was therefore only through this exchange announcement that the State that owns was made aware of what had been done, directly by the company. As of 2 April, it is fairly well known what has happened, not least through full coverage in the press. The main reason for the motion of censure lies, at least for parts of the opposition, in what happened before 2 April. That's why there's every reason to stop here. The State has anchored the governance of its ownership through the ownership notification, the share company law and the Norwegian recommendation for ownership management of the company and the country’s principles of good ownership. A central principle is that ownership decisions are taken at a general meeting. It is the company's duty to inform the owner, either through the Executive Director or the Chairperson, of issues relevant to the owner. The representative of the State Governing Board does not replace the contact of the company directly with the Ministry. A shareholder agreement such as the one that exists here will not replace but will also replace the principles that apply. The State has extensive ownership of many listed companies and has extensive experience in enforcing this type of information and processes. Very well known, the lack of information exchange between companies and the owner has not been a problem in the past. In this case, on the basis of the information available, it should be reasonable to conclude that the company did not wish or wish to share this information with the owner, much less discuss the actual content. The argument that the company itself argues, namely that it is the responsibility of the member of the board to keep the owner informed, is, in my opinion, thin. Because even if Aker ASA’s understanding of how the information flow was going, through the government’s representative, it did not provide the board representative with comprehensive information within five days before it was informed of the matter in Aker Holding’s board meeting. On the contrary, it seems to me that a good deal of resources have been used by the company to find out that there is no need for a treatment in the general meeting of Aker Solutions ASA, and therefore a decision-making matter in the board of Aker Holding AS. This has been consulted by two external advisers from Aker Solutions. Moreover, both state advisers conclude that this was a matter for the general meeting. In view of this, the opposition's criticism of the Council of State is therefore strange. The assumption for it is that this was available information, which could thus form the basis for the possibility ‘to engage an independent financial advisor to assess the strategy and price of the transactions before the adoption of the decision.’;» This could in turn provide the country with the basis for assessing the price and industrial strategy of buying-in on an independent basis before taking a decision. It is demanding to involve a financial advisor to review transactions for which no information is available, and then to use this as a basis for making own price and industrial strategy assessments. This is, according to the opinion of the Center Party, criticism that falls on its own unreasonableness. It is not less demanding when we see the approach of the Party of Advancement: first, they criticize – together with the other parties – the Council of State for being too passive, so – in a separate remark – for being too active. To use a metaphor from meteorology, state councils are exposed here to north wind from all sides. Governments have received unusual scratches from many teams for handling after 2 April. In the setting, this tone is slightly down. It is alarming in itself when we take into account both the strength and intensity of the criticism that came. The party of progress and Øyvind Korsberg thought that Brustad had to get out of the snow hole and clean up, Right and Torbjørn Hansen thought that the council could possibly be set up for national law, although it was a little unclear which laws she might have broken, while Left and Sponheim satisfied themselves with metaphors within the plant life and talking about themselves in the third person together with the prime minister. Not only is the criticism strongly muted, it is also given a whole new content as the case has evolved. Now it is as well-known lack of handling in advance that is the problem. The only opposition is able to gather around is the very mistrust proposal and – together with us – the need for an active state ownership, where the state that owns takes a clear, well-founded and independent position related to major strategic industrial dispositions in the company. The last one is okay with it, all the time it's traditionally the government parties that have arranged for such an exercise of state ownership. And if this is the opposition's power collection before the fall's elections, I track four new years for sitting government. In my opinion, therefore, there is little doubt that this is only a game in which the aim is to hit a council of states that has done little other than its job, a council of states that has been in the storm and has respected the values of the community and the rights of the company. Nothing has been achieved, says the opposition. Well, valuations are quality assured by the outside party. The transactions were the subject of proceedings at the general meeting and the shareholder agreement is to be amended to avoid these types of events in the future. It is easy for the Center Party to support Sylvia Brustad in this matter. I would add for myself that I agree with Mr Eriksen in Aker ASA when he writes in a letter to the Committee that ‘large loads could have been avoided if Aker had actively had the effect that Aker Solutions itself was a party so that the transactions were presented to the general meeting’. It is extremely important that transactions between related companies in the same sphere are carried out in a way that does not allow questions to be asked about them, as we have seen here. For the state that owns it, in my opinion, there are two factors that are crucial. Firstly, the state must always be able to demonstrate that it maintains its values in a good way. Whether values are lost or confused, the community's resources are disappearing, and those who make the values available have made it, not directly or deliberately, but through democratic choices and tax payments. This thus becomes a matter of particular concern to the State. Secondly, the State must always ensure that the interests of all shareholders are safeguarded – not just the big ones, or that individuals enrich themselves at the expense of the joint stock. I cannot help but understand that this is exactly what Sylvia Brustad has done in this matter, by getting a new review of valuations and getting the matter dealt with in the general meeting. So do we have something to learn from this from our side? Yes, and I think that both the Council of State and the Committee have taken good care of that. There is a need to clarify expectations and procedures in relation to the exchange of information. Similarly, the dialogue between owners improves, so that there is agreement before and not after the transactions are advertised. It's also important to put in the margin that noise of the size we've seen here is not good for the company. The focus in the company is on value creation, jobs and development, and the fact that you get a completely different focus over time is obvious from the very important job that needs to be done every day in companies of this size. This is a responsibility of all parties to a ownership relationship, and the country will always have at least as large process and documentation requirements as other owners, for the above reasons. Mistletoe is not a game. In addition to national court action, mistrust proposals are the biggest and most potent weapon of the Storting against the executive, both to the single state councils and to the government as a whole. Sometimes mistrust proposals have changed Norwegian political history. The classic example is the mistrust proposal by the minority in the municipal committee and the forestry, watercourse and industrial committee against the government of Gerhardsen on 23 August 1963, following the mining accident in Kings Bay at Svalbard. That proposal was adopted by 76 votes to 74, and led to the government applying for dismissal. Since then, Norwegian policies have been permanently changed. The Lyng government showed that the Workers' Party was not meant to rule forever. That motion of censure is one of over 60 since World War II. It was a real mistrust proposal, taken seriously, promoted in the context of a serious case – and it had consequences. Today’s motion of censure will not be a watershed in Norwegian political history – at best it will be a historical bracket. To the best of my ability, I've looked for parallels, something to compare with. My stable tip is that it comes in the same division as several of the mistrust proposals that the representative Carl I. Hagen has promoted. On 19 November 1974 he promoted a motion of censure against the Minister for Foreign Affairs on the presentation of the Minister for Foreign Affairs. It was rejected against one voice – probably Hagen himself. After the Progress Party contributed to the capture of the Willoch government, Hagen obviously tried to make it right through a series of mistrust proposals against the government of Brundtland. He put forward the first in the line already during the throne debate on 20-21. October 1986. The proposal was, as we know, not accepted, against 2 votes. It is therefore new from that edge when Carl I. Hagen says that mistrust proposals should not be promoted in vain. So when will the Stortinge promote distrust proposals? Yes, when the Government has breached the duty of information to Parliament, it has acted contrary to the will of the Storting, the leaders and the law. We cannot escape the word'serious' in this context. The mistletoe against the Brustad Government Council will be briefly measured against all these criteria. First of all, it seems tailor-made for the media, and beyond the Storting's duty to act seriously, seriously and even decently. At the same time, such a proposal itself requires serious reflection and careful consideration. For my part, I have come to realize that I could not have supported it, even though I had represented an opposition party. It's too thin, too unprofessional and too difficult to take seriously. Hamsun wrote that in a hundred years everything is forgotten – well to notice except that very sentence. Today's motion of censure will be gone from the collective memory in good time before a hundred years have passed. Understand, who knows? Perhaps it is an example of meaningless and stillborn mistrust proposals, just like Hagen's mistrust proposals against the Minister of Foreign Affairs in 1974. It's not going to be written important political history here today. We're light years away from the Kings Bay case. Responsibility lies with the opposition. I'm sure they've done the best they could. That's their excuse. It's also their verdict, to quote one of the foremost people who's had their work here in this room.

Then back to the Aker case: Already on 18 September 2008, through its representative on the board, the state became aware of plans for internal transactions in Aker. A number of meetings later were given further information about this. The state did nothing to stop, prevent, investigate or get more information about this before the matter became known through an exchange message from Aker on 2 April, and in the media the next day. At that time, decisions were taken and agreed to be signed – quite legally and legitimately, this would prove to be the case. After a few days of press noise, Minister of Industry Sylvia Brustad is visibly awakening on 5 April and is conducting an independent investigation. Two days later, Brustad says that she believes the transactions between Aker and Aker Solutions must be cancelled. The State, through the Minister of Industry, also engaged the company Pareto to review the financial aspects of the transactions. Despite the fact that both of these reports conclude that the transactions were illegal and the valuation was too high, the State and the State Council of Brustad unilaterally chose to focus on Aker’s own report from the company USB Investment Bank, which concluded that the transactions had been carried out ‘fair’. Why the Easter Councils were ‘cursed and frustrated’, carried out investigations of both the legality of the decisions, through Selmer, and of the valuation, through Pareto, and then not as if the conclusions of these self-initiated external investigations were still an unanswered question. What we're left with is a Council of States that has, in fact, gone from being “cursed”, to laying down the toad surface for Smoke and Aker, and trying to present it as a saying that it became a general assembly – a general assembly in which the state, first of all, was completely uninterested, and in which, secondly, it was not moved on a comma in the agreements that have been concluded. What can we learn from this matter? For me, there are some things that will be important in the future. Government purchases result in a significant concentration of power at the hands of the state. This leads to unfair conditions, by the state sitting on several sides of the table at the same time. That's why at least we have to have fundamental debates in advance of a purchase of this type. There is a great difference between ownership of these types of companies, with their culture, and ownership of the traditional state enterprises, where there are obvious historical and political reasons for state ownership. I want to stop by what has been a central point in the debate, namely the question of communication and what the representative Foss called two-track communication. I do not think it is clear enough in the debate that the state's acquisition in Aker – the state as a minority shareholder in Aker together with one large and dominant majority shareholder – is a fundamental new feature in state ownership. We should remember that most of the state ownership, which the Ministry of Economic Affairs manages – and which I have also enjoyed for a period of time, as Minister of Industry – concerns companies where the state is established as owner. For a long time, the country has been the owner, it is historically justified and tradition of having the country as its owner. I can confirm what has been said about ownership in these types of companies, that there is a significant informal link between the management of the companies and the Ministry of Industry – and, very often, the Minister of Industry personally. It's just to mention Hydro, Statoil, Telenor and other companies. Here group management and chairman are very alert in the particular situation the state is in as owner, and make sure to flow to very much information. I have to say that sometimes I thought it was alarming to see how much commitment they could show – that at an early stage they almost shared their thoughts about what one might think about in the future, and that could possibly become a burden to the state as the owner. I could have spent my entire speaking time today telling stories about this. So these are companies with an established culture to have the state as their owner. There is a broad understanding of how to deal with it, and it has not been necessary to formalise it. The state goes into Aker as its owner, along with Røkke. Some aspects should be clear to the State – not least from the experience of the Ministry of Economic Affairs as having competence in different contexts relating to the Aker and Smoke systems, acquisition proceedings, acquisitions of different companies, etc. – when you involve yourself with him as an owner, and with his way of operating companies. I would like to stress that I have never seen anything other than that Smoke is staying on the right side of the law – but not anymore. Millimeters are used. And neither he nor his companies, I think, spend a lot of time worrying about the problems the state could have with its ownership. This is knowledge The Ministry of Economic Affairs has at the time when you were to be part of this ownership. And then it strikes me: one alternative is that the representative Hagen is absolutely right. The state, with the knowledge and experience it had, did not choose to formalise this because it did not intend to exercise the kind of ownership that is done in other companies in which the state owns. One had simply intended to carry out what I believe is a political justification for this ownership and this acquisition – and, incidentally, Smoke allowed to continue to create values and build companies. Then came this situation, where it turned out that the state and the Minister of Industry obviously needed to carry out a type of activity that I don't think was meant to be when they established this ownership. The second option is that the State, with all the knowledge it has, and the whole new situation to go into, in this case by being a minority shareholder, must, of course, make sure to formalise this, if it is possible to do so in a shareholder agreement, in order to obtain a special treatment, as this actually is, by ensuring that the State has wide dialogue and in-depth contact with the company – or otherwise ensure that the formal or informal contact between the company and the department, i.e. the State that owns directly outside the board, would take place. I don't think one had reason to believe that the smoking system and the Aker would do like Hydro, Statoil and all the other companies would have done. It must have been written in mountains – and read up almost every day – in order to ensure that it had been established. And in that case, the neglect is that you didn't do it. So I'm going to end by pointing to the reason I have to call this the biggest scandal in this period. It is, in isolation, not the government council Brustad's handling of Easter hours, but the whole race, which ended with the state going in and paying scarcely NOK 5 billion to Smoke, without smoking's power – it's well documented lately – has been somewhat degraded. He can continue as before with these companies, but with a considerable amount of money in his pocket. He's got what he's always wanted, I think, a minority shareholder with a lot of money – and can still retain the power he needs. There's been a political game around this purchase. I sat as the parliamentary leader in this house when in January 2007 I received a telephone – I have mentioned this earlier from this pulpit – from a furious smoke, which was put on the sideline of the merger negotiations between Statoil and Hydro. He scolded out the red-green government and thought he was entitled to a better treatment, because he had so actively supported the Workers' Party during the election campaign in 2005. He said this in clear text on the phone. He also said something similar to the other parliamentary leaders. After that round, the noise and the attention that was about Smoking's obvious dissatisfaction that he did not receive any political special treatment, it was silent for a few months – until at the end of June we got the news that Andersen, along with other good friends in LO and business, had negotiated this agreement, and the state came in as the owner. It shall be argued long and well before I will believe that this purchase did not have a strong political justification. I think it's quite well documented in what happened around Easter times, under the poor governance and management of the Brustad Council, that one was so concerned with achieving the political justification that the solid work of the Ministry of Economic Affairs almost always does in such cases, one had no time to do in that case. One was to achieve the policy and was not so terribly concerned with the store.
ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e1058	The state is a large owner in Norwegian business. Many of the companies in which the State owns shares have world-leading competence and, through their presence and management in Norway, contribute to maintaining an innovative and competitive business. This also applies to Aker Solutions, a leading global supplier of advanced technological solutions. The company is well ahead, for example: in the case of the provision of solutions for the collection of petroleum at high sea depths and under challenging climatic conditions. A significant element of Norwegian long-term ownership helps to ensure that companies have their head office and research activities in Norway. It's of great importance. At the head office of Aker Solutions, decisions are taken of major importance for the company's business and strategic development and for the development of other Norwegian-based companies that are subcontractors of this company. It is in this context that we need to see the state ownership of Aker Holding, which is anchored in Stortinget by the decision of 11 December 2007, where the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Trade was authorised to complete a purchase of 30 pst. of the shares in Aker Holding. The ownership of Aker Holding ensures, as is known to the State, through a shareholder agreement, negative control over some important issues, such as: location of Aker Solutions’ head office, seagoing with a relatively small holding of 12 pst. On 2 April of this year, Aker Solutions published the five extensively mentioned share transactions and a loan transaction. The Aker ASA group was a party to all these transactions. The total volume was significant. The stock market reacted negatively to the news of the transactions. On the basis of the information available on 2 April, I stated that the Ministry felt that the decision-making process should be different, and, as I had previously also informed the Supervisory and Constitutional Committee, I asked for three things: Firstly, on behalf of the state, I wanted an independent review of the transactions. Secondly, I asked that the transactions in the meantime be suspended. Thirdly, I asked that this matter be dealt with at the general meeting, so that all the shareholders could have their say, because this is also about protecting shareholder democracy. If this could not be fulfilled, I thought the transactions had to be reversed. In the solution that now exists between Aker, Saab, Investor and the State, these conditions are taken care of. So the solution that now exists meets the requirements that we set. I would also like to stress that I have never at any time expressed any idea of the price or strategy behind the transactions at this stage. I note that in the attitude of the Committee on Control and Constitutional Affairs, an overall committee refers to the need for active and conscious ownership by the State. I fully agree with that, and it is in line with the government's ownership policy as expressed in St.meld. No 13 for 2006–2007, An active and long-term ownership. I have also noticed that the committee believes the matter illustrates the need to clarify the state's expectations regarding information from the companies. The experience of the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Trade over the years is that this has not been an ongoing problem. However, in the light of this case, it is natural to review this. Three of the four owners of Aker Holding AS – the State, Saab and Investor as well as board members both in Aker Holding AS and in Aker Solutions ASA – asked questions about the decision-making process. Furthermore, Aker’s own audit committee pointed out on 14 April this year that they would like to see these transactions better anchored. It is the company of the Executive Director and the Management Board which is responsible for ensuring that the proceedings are properly handled, and it is the responsibility of the company to inform and, where appropriate, also involve the owners so that they can take care of their interests. The Board of Directors is an office of the company, and a board member is the trustee of the company and represents the entire shareholder community, even when the representative is elected by the state. The company cannot therefore assume that a matter is anchored with the State only the member of the board has been elected by the State and should have been informed. In other words, they meet with a member of the board of directors and do not replace the contact between the Executive Director and the Chairman of the board to have with the shareholders on behalf of the company. I record that there has been criticism of the solution we are now agreeing on, among other things. having regard to the fact that the proceedings of the general meeting were apparently not to be effective since the State had decided in advance and the processing took place after the transactions had been completed. First of all, I would say that it is a matter of course that the owners of Aker Holding, that is Aker, Saab, Investor and the State, had agreed in advance on how Aker Holding, as the largest shareholder in Aker Solutions, would vote at the General Assembly. It was just supposed to be missing! This does not mean that the processing of the transactions by the general meeting was insignificant, on the contrary. When Aker Solutions and other companies have investigations with large owners prior to general meetings, it is precisely to avoid the type of situations where large owners vote for important proposals. This is the case in other major cases, such as: the separation of Yara from Hydro, by bringing together Hydro's oil business with Statoil, through buy-back programmes of its own shares in several of the listed companies, etc. Secondly, that the processing took place after the transactions had been completed, I would say that we would have liked the General Assembly of Aker Solutions to deal with the transactions before they were completed – it was said. That is exactly what is at the heart of this matter. I want to show that Aker Solutions in a press release on 20 April wrote that until a new review of independent advisors was ready, ‘the enterprises purchased from the Aker ASA Group will be maintained as independent entities. Aker ASA, on its side, has accepted that the settlement of the shares in the relevant companies Aker Oilfield Services, Aker DOF Deepwater, Odim and Midsund Use will not take place until the end of May». Now the transactions known to have been approved by the General Assembly, and we are pleased with that. I think everyone can agree that we would have preferred to be in addition to the situation we've been in in the last few months. However, as is well known, the state is a major owner of a large number of companies, and state ownership is very important for the Norwegian economy, for Norwegian jobs and for Norwegian business. The Ministry manages holdings in 20 companies. The ministry manages on behalf of the state values for several hundred billion dollars. That's why we have to be very clear about where the boundaries go between what companies can and should handle on their own, and what things we as owners are going to be involved in and decide in. When situations arise where there is doubt about where this limit goes, we have to take the job it is to clean up, sail if it is demanding for everyone involved. I am pleased that we now have a solution, and that the case has been submitted to the owners, as requested by both Saab, the Investor and the State. Aker Solutions is one of the most important industrial and technology companies in Norway. I am now committed to looking forward and contributing to the continued positive development of this company in the interests of Norwegian jobs. Through national ownership, we ensure that this important company will also be operated from Norway in the future.

They're doing well because, essentially, we've been busy being long-term, professional, and entrusting the big decisions to those who're going to take them. The Progress Party does not have a fundamental counter-representation against the state being the owner of different companies, if it serves our common interest. But the main goal must be to maximise the values on behalf of taxpayers, because that's because of their money we've gone into this ownership. This is a motion of censure which I believe the position should take far more seriously than they have done in this debate, because it is about the very critical office of the Council of State. That's what this is about. I have to say that I think it becomes more than clear through this debate that the Council of State has subsequently tried to construct an explanation to keep water in public space, to expound its own actions along the way. It becomes more and more obvious to me. There is no doubt that the meeting on 13 March was familiar with the principle of the matter. You were familiar with 80-90 pst. of the transactions involved — and they did not respond. Governments responded only after media pressure — only after the case received considerable public attention. Then the council found out that it was time to respond. In my opinion, that's not how you manage a professional ownership. It's not when you stand in the north wind and it blows from all sides you suddenly realize that you have to change the strategy in your ownership exercise. It is absolutely legitimate to want to change the strategy along the way, but then you have to have a well-founded explanation of why you do it. But then you choose to do it at a very different time than you did in this case. I think it must be at the heart of the state's exercise of ownership in all companies that one is predictable, long-term, credible and stable. There is no doubt that many of the statements made by the Council of State in this matter damaged the company – and that is not a good exercise of ownership. But let me say a few words about state ownership in general, because one of the mantras of the red-green parties in the election campaign in 2005 was: Now there was going to be a completely different kind of state ownership, now we were going to get active exercise of state ownership – now it was going to be really great! Well, what we've seen now is a very embarrassing notion – if that's what the red-green parties mean by active state ownership. What is important — precisely because the state manages such large values — is that you are predictable in the ownership you exercise. It may be that in some companies you want to exercise a passive ownership, which was the plan of Aker Holding. It was quite obvious that you intended to ensure head office location, but besides that, you really didn't want to have so many opinions about the company's operation. It's totally legitimate. The Progress Party supported it. But the truth is that Sylvia Brustad and the government in retrospect – when they were under pressure – needed to think differently. It's not very professional! It is also possible to want to have a much more active ownership in other companies, but my point is that you have to stick to the predictable strategy all the way, for each company, and not suddenly change it when the wind starts blowing a little too strongly around you, and you need to find an explanation that fits the current situation. I believe that the Government for the future would stand to define a little more clearly what they mean by active state ownership, simply because it is about managing significant values in large, important companies in a way that taxpayers in this country have earned, that helps to increase values, and that efforts are being made to involve, as far as possible, substituting political arguments in the exercise of ownership.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e1678	In 2007, the State acquired 30 pst. of the axans of Aker Holding AS. It's well-known, not least after the debate we've had this morning.
