ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e1689	The debate on the government's handling of the Aker Holding case this winter has been held, and so has the debate on the purchase of these shares in Aker Holding in 2007. The reason why the Right takes up the proposal in the vote to wind up Aker Holding is that there is a possibility of such liquidation in the shareholder agreement. It is mentioned directly in the shareholder agreement. It appears that the company may be wound up if the shareholders so agree. The right considers that the experience with ownership in Aker Holding clearly points to the fact that this type of state ownership structure is unfortunate for several reasons. Let me first stress that the Right is not the principal opponent of all state ownership. The State has direct holdings in a number of companies, and the Right has no plans to sell out all these shareholdings. Nor do we support the proposal in the vote to sell the shares in Aker Solutions in the situation we have in Norwegian business today. But for the Right, it's very important that state ownership is professional and tidy. Professional State ownership is necessary in order to ensure the sound management of Community financial resources. A professional state ownership must exist in order to ensure sound ownership management in relation to other shareholders of companies within the country. A professional state ownership is important to ensure a stable framework around important activities and jobs in Norway. The exercise of professional ownership is not always straightforward. The exercise of ownership by the State is from time to time debated, also for the companies in which the State has direct ownership. However, I would like to stress that the state has quite a degree of respect for its professionalism in the exercise of ownership, through a competent ownership department. The problem with Aker Holding is that this part of ownership in the arrangement deviates fundamentally from the direct ownership items that the State is used to managing. Instead of normal ownership through participation in general meetings and electoral committees, the State has indirect ownership here. You have a shareholder agreement, and you have a directly elected board representative in Aker Holding. Of course, it is commendable that the Supervisory and Constitutional Committee now considers that there is a need to clarify the country’s expectations for information in relation to this ownership. But it also shows that the proceedings in this ownership structure appear unclear to the Government. Unclearness in corporate governance is very unfortunate. In my view, Aker ASA has a long way to go in interpreting the terms of the shareholder agreement regarding the proceedings and information to the shareholders. The right considers that the State must participate in ownership in a much clearer way. In addition to confusion, there are several serious objections to this ownership structure. First, the State transfers control of the shares owned by the State to another shareholder. This gives this other shareholder greater power over the underlying corporations than the shareholder's own shareholding should be. There is no doubt that this transfer of power is unfortunate. It's just the exercise of this power of ownership that's left to another shareholder, which is the background for the whole Aker Holding case that we're dealing with instead. It is also the case that the binding agreement between the shareholders of Aker Holding locks the country’s ownership position in a way that weakens its values. The fact that the State cannot dispose of its own shares in certain situations can cause an economic loss to the State. It is a device that in the stock market normally gives a so-called stock discount. At the same time, this special device gives a rather incorrect impression of the protection afforded by this ownership for activities and jobs in Aker Solutions. It is obvious that the main owner has an influence which allows both major changes in the underlying activities of the enterprise, relocation of jobs out of the country and large withdrawals from this company without any intervention by the State as a shareholder. Thus, the idea is that the locking mechanism ensures the workplaces, relatively illusory. Therefore, the right considers that all shareholders of Aker Holding could benefit from the fact that Aker Holding was wound up, so that the owners had a direct ownership in the usual way. This would also be quite positive for the other minority shareholders in Aker Solutions, because such a clean-up and strengthening of the corporate structure could provide a better value for the entire company. All in all, I believe that the experience with the Aker Holding investment, both in 2007 and what has taken place now in 2009, clearly confirms that this type of ownership structure should remain far away from the State. I also think that part of the government parties see that, but otherwise it would be very interesting to know whether it could have been relevant for this government to enter into the corresponding purchase and agreement with other owners in the Norwegian business community.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e1724	The Christian People's Party stresses that national ownership is important in some cases to ensure that companies have head offices and research activities in Norway. The State and Aker ASA on their side entered into an agreement with ten years of commitment. Aker ASA's sale of five companies to Aker Solutions AS, a subsidiary of Aker Solutions ASA, at a value of approximately NOK 2 billion was carried out with effect on 2 April this year, and it has just been treated in this room. The government's problem with managing the internal transactions is an indication that this is a complex structure for the state, by being a minority owner in a holding company of a listed company together with a dominant private owner. The Christian People's Party believes that the model chosen by the state strengthens the influence of a selected major shareholder in the underlying company, with the challenges it poses when it concerns corporate governance and internal transactions. The state's investment in Aker Holding is not in itself an obstacle to the sale, relocation and conversion of the underlying companies, and it is uncertain whether the investment ensures jobs. On the contrary, it has been claimed that the purchases made increase the financial obligations of the underlying group. On the other hand, the State has a long experience of direct ownership as the owner model for such large shareholdings. Such a model is more neat, and it is transparent when it relates to power structure. By the fact that Aker, the Swedish owners and the state become the direct owners of Aker Solutions ASA, the owners will be able to exercise the influence of the actual ownership and provide the company with a more normal governance structure. Better corporate governance will be positive for the values of an listed entity. Against this background, the Christian People's Party supports the proposal to wind up Aker Holding and replace the indirect ownership with direct ownership of Aker Solutions.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e1881	This matter has been raised by the Committee's minority of one third, as a matter of its own, inter alia. after consultation and public attention of Uniteam AS. This matter has been going on in the media for a long time, because Uniteam has actually been boycotted by his most important customer, the defense, after the defense had also reported Uniteam for various matters, since Uniteam should have had preferential transfers and Uniteam should have tried to trick the defense – that is, fraud of the Defense. The story we got from Uniteam was quite different from what has happened in the public space. To this end, the Committee decided to open up the case and took views in a number of letters to the Minister of Defence. We also received enquiries from Uniteam. It was also decided to hold a hearing. I refer to the setting, where the record of what was said in the inspection hearing has been included. What is quite clear is that the defense looks at some purchases from Uniteam of some containers, first in 2002 and then new container purchases in 2003, completely different from the production from Uniteam. The defence claims that this was the case in 2003, was a single supplier situation, which was subject to a very special regulation, where Uniteam was allowed to have a profit margin of less than 1 pst, regardless of the fact that there was a contract of more than 100 million. kr, and that there was a very high risk involved. It's the defense's manufacture. They would then come to a meeting where this was to be clarified and signed. This did not happen, but they were told to start producing these containers, and the contract amount, the final amount, was accepted by the Defence. Then they were told that they had to draw up and sign some special forms, so that the paper and documentable was in order for the defence's internal systems. They got some new forms, which they had to sign and correct. They were told that this was only pro forma, to satisfy the defense's internal systems. The price of the containers was the same all the time, and the defense had accepted it. But there had to be some calculations for this where the cost was distributed in a different way. They were not told, they believe, that it was an exclusive supplier agreement, subject to very special things. This defence has not been at all close to being able to document. So there are two different presentations of the case. The breach of contract on the part of the defence had been raised in the committee as a matter of its own. What we're facing is a review. There are two owners of Uniteam, not the company itself. The study reveals incorrect use of residential facilities, lack of day-time activities, low medical coverage in hospitals, deficiencies in systems of anomalies management and insufficiently anchored cooperation agreements with hospitals. The National Audit Office emphasises that a number of measures have been put in place by the Ministry to strengthen the provision of care and care services for municipalities in the context of the 2015 care plan. The number of annual works has increased — in particular between 2005 and 2007 — by about 11,000 annual works, which the Riksrevision writes is due to the strong commitment of the government and the increase. In addition, the Government has introduced investment grants for the establishment of places with day-to-day care and measures for increased manning capacity and enhanced professional competence and has put in place a number of measures for unskilled people. Furthermore, the Ministry has required municipalities to introduce a local standard for medical treatment in hospitals and has increased demands for day care. Municipalities themselves are responsible for ensuring that they have a professionally good care service that is sufficient to meet the needs of citizens for services. In the Questionnaire in Stortinget, we got an explanation of this promise from the Prime Minister, that it was a promise not to reduce the level of taxation. The 2005 election campaign declaration that one should have 10,000 new hands in the care of the elderly, and that one should not have any tax relief before the care of the elderly, has now been translated into maintaining the quality of care for the elderly at the same level as in 2005. The other one promised was that one would not have any tax relief during this period. I hardly think that's what the voters heard Stoltenberg say. Today we stand with the fasit. What he promised he would keep. But I don't think it's a good idea to try to make it something other than what it is.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e2408	As the rapporteur for the annual report of the National Audit Office in the last four years, I would just like to make a few comments on the role of the National Audit Office in society. There has been something that has been bothering me since I first came here in 1974. Then I made proposals for the 1975 government budget – alone, without advisers and without colleagues. It was quite polite. It was the first time I strengthened the Riksrevision, because I wanted to increase control of the administration. I'm glad that since then the Riksrevision has been substantially strengthened and has been given a completely different place in the Norwegian community. Through some reforms in 1992 and the setting-up of the Control and Constitutional Committee in 1993, the National Audit Office was also given more access to and required to provide administrative audits, as well as Document No. 1st@item: inlistbox I think the development of administrative audits from the National Audit Office has been positive. Now they're going to be a number from 12 to 15 per. year and handled by the Control and Constitutional Committee and by the Stortinget. The role of the National Audit Board in relation to the control apparatus has been significantly strengthened in the time I have had the pleasure of being in Stortingen, without saying that I have had something special to do here in this room. But I think it's an observation that I, as the case-rapporteur for the Riksaudition Annual Reports, want to produce. I hope that the Riksrevision will continue to be strengthened, because as the administration, the government – the executive – is getting more and more power to decide matters of great importance for both companies and residents, it is vital that we have a control system and sound management. At the same time as giving increased power to the government, it is necessary to strengthen the National Audit Office, strengthen the control apparatus, which is also the Civil Ombudsman, and in recent times, the work of the secret services, the EOS Committee, so that there is a balance in society between those exercising power and the control apparatus, in order to prevent as much abuse of power as possible. In the Frøiland Committee, I was delighted to make suggestions to change the composition of the Riksrevision. I think it might be in place with a National Auditor who is appointed independently of the political, and who can't really be dismissed, in order to ensure the independence of the National Auditor. A party-politically appointed national audit, in which the largest party usually receives the head, i.e. the permanent employee and the executive director of the National Audit Office, may in reality cause problems with party-political ties. My personal reflections are that so far there has been no abuse in this area. Those who have held the position of national auditors have acted politically neutrally. They have acted as inspectors, regardless of personal and party political views. It's vital. I hope therefore, of course, that we will not have any ties or reactions in the choice of the National Audit Board that might create suspicion that someone wants a national audit that is somewhat more obedient and somewhat less independent than what we have experienced in recent years. As the rapporteur for the annual reports of the National Audit Office in recent years, I wanted to make these general points of view known. I also refer to the Committee's position.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e2465	Mozambique is among the poorest countries in the world, despite a reduction in the proportion of the poor and an annual growth in the economy of around 8pst. Norway has had formal development cooperation with Mozambique since 1977, and the central objective of Norwegian aid has been poverty reduction. The present report from the Riksrevision has been prepared in cooperation between the national audits in Norway, Denmark and Sweden under the direction of the Norwegian National Audit Office. It is part of greater cooperation, the HOAP, between national audits in a number of countries providing development assistance. The Group was set up to harmonise and coordinate audits of development assistance through a common audit practice and information sharing. This type of cooperation projects contributes to both the coordination, harmonisation and transfer of knowledge. Norway is one of the nineteen countries participating in a cooperation on general budget support for Mozambique. All donors participating, signed a joint intention agreement in 2004, and a new agreement is signed this year. Aid to Mozambique amounted to a total of USD 1429 in 2007, i.e. approx. 50 pst. by the State budget of the country. About 1⁄4 of this is given as budget support. The study has focused in particular on the fulfilment by donors of their obligations regarding the transfer of funds and further transfer to Mozambique’s central bank account, the use and follow-up of reports and the quality of the data contained in the reports and the anti-corruption theme. The study shows that there are positive results in several areas. This is a positive development in the country’s economy and management systems, and donors have complied with their commitments in 2007. The accounts of the Bank of Mozambique show that the transfers are converted into local currency at the correct exchange rate and made available in a timely manner to the Ministry of Finance. The appointed auditors have carried out the work in line with their mandate. Three key areas for potential improvements are highlighted: increased use of electronic financial management systems, more active use of reports from the Mozambican audit institutions and closer cooperation with them, and follow-up of the quality of statistics to enhance performance monitoring. However, although the quality of financial reporting has improved, it does not provide the providers with sufficient information. This is due, inter alia, to: that only approx. 1⁄4 of the State budget is part of the electronic accounting system and that wage payments are not included. Corruption is also a major problem in Mozambique. The report notes that the basis for the fight against corruption has been laid, but that few results have been achieved so far and that the targets for 2007 have not been met. Work against corruption is very important. Corruption reduces efforts to combat poverty and also weakens trust in governments. In its opinion, an overall committee stresses that the Ministry of Foreign Affairs must address the issue of corruption both in direct contact with the authorities in Mozambique and in cooperation with the other donor countries. The progress part and the right part have some own comments in the setting. Here they are, among others. critical to increased budget support and multiannual commitments. The Christian People's Party agrees that budget support is a major challenge in terms of control. At the same time, however, countries receiving support are able to learn to do their own priorities and to manage their own budgets more effectively.
ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e2482	The rapporteur has explained the position in a good way, and I would also like to say that we think it is positive the work that the Riksaudition carried out, in cooperation with others. It is important that assistance in general, but we think budget support in particular, is subject to this type of control. After all, from time to time, it has been revealed that we believe we have been involved in, after all, improving the way we provide assistance – that gives us some knowledge. Some of that knowledge is, among other things, that, unfortunately, conditions do not always improve in management, in governance. That things are going forward in Mozambique, we are happy, and that's a picture that fortunately stands out for more countries in Africa. However, corruption was also addressed by the rapporteur, and I am pleased that the committee has said something about it. But what's going to be the question on our side is how to follow this up. It is said in the answer given by the Ministry of Foreign Affairs: 'The theme, according to the Ministry, is central to the follow-up and political dialogue with the authorities.' But what are the requirements? Where's the limit going? How long can we wait for a country like Mozambique not to deliver? Because they don't deliver, it's pretty clear from this setting. ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e2489	Let me first say that Mozambique is one of Africa's great success stories in the last 15 years. After one of Africa's most bloody civil wars, both Frelimo and Renamo, the two parties – the fronts of the civil war – are now sitting together in Parliament and fighting their fights peacefully. It is one of the countries that are still receiving the premium on an African scale for democratic change from one head of state to another, which is not a matter of course in many African countries, and it is one of those countries in Africa that have had the highest economic growth in the last 10–15 years, albeit from an extremely low point of view. So if you are going to choose a country that is an obvious candidate for budget support, Mozambique will undoubtedly be very high on that list: democratic, peaceful, politically stable and with economic progress. But if we are to assess budget support at all, and it was out of the Paris Declaration to which the Stortinget had a positive relationship, where donors are to harmonise their behaviour, it is difficult to imagine any more obvious candidate in Africa than Mozambique. That being said, it is fantastic that the National Audit Office has taken up this work, and not least that it has coordinated itself with national audits in other countries. It is precisely the type of harmonised work from the transducers that will give greater weight when the results are presented. Of course, we will follow this up through our Embassy in Maputo and in cooperation with all the other donors involved in both bilateral programmes and budget support in Mozambique. Norway is an international driving force in addressing this in all contexts – I address these issues in a series of multilateral and bilateral contexts. No later than today, the so-called Capital Refugees Committee presented its report, and we are waiting for a wide-ranging debate on it and on what measures it should lead to, both in poor countries and in Norway. Because there is a coordinated international economic system that makes corruption possible. Within that framework, of course, we will also address corruption issues in Mozambique with other donors. It is a key issue for Norway in any dialogue with other donors, and we will address it with the authorities of Mozambique with clear demands, and see it as a matter of course that all aid programmes, budget support, bilateral programmes and for that part programmes through non-governmental organisations have zero tolerance for corruption, even in countries where corruption is a major part, a dangerous part, of the social structure.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e2510	Let me first say that if you look at world statistics, you see that there's almost a one-to-one relationship between high location on Transparency International's corruption index, that is, a country is very corrupt, and low location according to all economic criteria. The government-appointed Equal pay Commission has reviewed the issue of equal pay in Norway. Their main conclusion is that we have a sex-shared labour market in Norway, which leads to inequality in pay, because people with higher education who work in the public sector earn 80 cents where people with higher education who work in the private sector earn 1 cents. When it is also the case that women are the main candidates for jobs requiring higher education in the public sector, this issue also has a clear gender perspective. The gender perspective is reflected in the work of the Equal pay Commission, which draws two main conclusions: that we have a sex-shared labour market, where the main pay gap is between the public sector educational groups versus the private sector, and, as the minority in the Equal pay Commission states, that there is the front-line model, with the prioritisation of the low-wage groups in the private sector and the LO-organised in the 1980s and 1990s, which has in reality made the issue of equal pay a matter not of prioritizing the low-wage groups, as it was in the past, but of prioritising the educational groups. ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e3319	At the request of the Committee on Energy and the Environment, the President will propose that the debate be limited to 1 hour and 5 minutes and that speaking time be allocated to the groups as follows: Working Party 20 minutes, Progress Party 15 minutes, Right 10 minutes, Socialist Left Party 5 minutes, Christian People's Party 5 minutes, Center Party 5 minutes and Left 5 minutes. ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e3330	This is a joyous day for Norway as an energy nation and a petroleum nation. Today, the Stortinge adopts a new important step in Norwegian oil history. Goliath is the first oil field planned to be developed in the Barents Sea, and that is an important step. The project is thus an important milestone for the development of the petroleum industry in the northern regions and will strengthen Norway’s reputation as an energy supplier to the world. The matter has been the subject of much debate, both on the future of Norway as an oil nation and on issues such as oil protection and local ring effects. It is therefore gratifying that a large majority on the Biggest stand together and give green light to this effort. In some areas, the Left and, in part, the Christian People's Party have differing views. It will give them the opportunity to explain in their articles. When both the SV and the Progress Party agree on the content of this setting, we know that the project is politically robust. I am pleased that SV has moved far in a positive direction to get a majority in this matter. The red-green government also notes in the proposition: ‘To maintain and increase the petroleum-related activity in the north, it is dependent on both exploration and field development in the region.’ This, in my view, signals both important objectives and a proper approach. However, for personal reasons, I wanted those objectives to be reflected in an oil notification, so that they were generally applicable and not just expressed in a proposition like this. In any case, the implementation and results of the Goliath project are important because the project of many will be considered a reference point in the further oil debate in Norway. In the Goliath project, therefore, the petroleum industry must demonstrate that they control the natural conditions. This must be borne in mind when they show economic management, technology choices, local ring-effects, environmental considerations and oil protection preparedness – in order to mention something. I say this to underline the seriousness and responsibility of the builder and operator to carry out the project in the best possible way and thus create a positive ground for further petroleum activities in the Barents Sea. Goliath is located 85 km northwest of Hammerfest and 50 km south-east of the Snow White field. Eni Norway is the operator of the field and has a holding of 65 pst. Statoil has a holding of 35 pst. The reserves in the field are about 174 million barrels of oil. That'll be what one initially has to extract. Gas is present in the field, approx. Eight billion Sm3. It's not big enough to be extractable in this round, at least not commercially, and will be reinjected for pressure support in the first round. At a later stage, the operator expects that the gas will also be produced and sold. The planned start of production is 2013, and the investments related to Goliath are estimated to exceed NOK 28 billion. In addition to the expansion of Snow White, the Goliath project will be the largest industrial project ever carried out in North Norway. It's also worth noting. Technically, Goliath shall be expanded with a liquid production unit with associated sea bottom wells. The device will comply with the requirements of the Barents Sea to ensure that no discharges of water produced into the sea during normal operation will occur. A fixed anchored liquid unit has been selected to adapt to current climatic conditions. In some political environments and in parts of the business world in the north, we know that there was a desire to impose landing requirements as a development solution for Goliath. The marginal profitability of the project cannot justify such a solution and it is a reality which the Committee has taken on board. This has not been a major debate on it either. The local ring effects are strongly emphasised in the setting. It is a general goal that the petroleum industry should benefit the whole country, which I think most people feel is happening. The oil business shall also produce local ring effects. With regard to the petroleum debate that has taken place in Norway, I believe that the oil companies should also note the desire for local ring-effects in order to create the acceptance for further development of the oil industry, especially in the north. Estimates suggest that the Goliath field can, including indirect employees, provide between 450 and 600 local and regional jobs during the operational phase. I'd call that a good thing. Eni Norway will establish a completely new regional office with operational functions, which will be added to Hammerfest. The Agency will subsequently have staff within, for example: engineering, geology, reservoir engineering and administrative personnel related to the operation of the Goliath field. It shows that this is more than a simple supply-base. It also takes care of the Committee's desire to ensure that there is real activity in the Hammerfest area of the Goliath field. There will also be a helicopter terminal and supply base being added to the area. These functions will include approx. 150-200 direct jobs. I would also like to mention that the operator will adapt the contract strategy to the maintenance and operational contracts and build up local and regional competences both through educational staff in companies and in secondary schools in different ways. They will inform and qualify local and regional suppliers through so-called supplier networks and will establish hosting arrangements for local suppliers in their own organisation. It is also worth noting that Eni Norway does not have framework agreements for its deployment and operation deliveries. It allows for the outsourcing of parts of the contracts to local and regional suppliers, which allow for the development of that industry. Having said that, as a single politician, I would like to express our hope that we avoid a copy of the Luteland approach after the Stortinget has dealt with the matter, where some people get an interest in complaining or appealing to some parts of the project, and that one does not treat it in the department, and we get quasi-solutions, as we have seen in the Gjøa project. Let's take the signals that have come from the committee, which clearly says that if there are similar anchors that we have seen on Luteland, it will be dealt with quickly and quickly and will not put the project at risk, so you can see that happened to the Luteland project. The energy supply project is planned to electrify from land. The Committee is in favour of this. At the same time, there are some assumptions that have to be met. Adequate shore-side power supply is involved. It is true that the costs of action here are proportionate to what is to be expected in the current climate policy and that the technical challenges are acceptable. Full electrification of Goliath will require withdrawal of more than 100 MW from the network. Studies of the power situation show that it will not be possible in the short term. So the power situation in Finnmark does not allow full electrification, and that's what the committee has taken over. It is important to avoid having a copy of the situation, for example: in central Norway, where industrial development is permitted without the electricity supply being included. Therefore, full electrification may last as long as 10 years before it takes place, but the plan is still in place. Goliath will start with a combination solution that will both have a gas turbine on the platform that generates some megawatts, and you will get electricity from countries that account for about half the total. 20-40 MW. Now I would also like to note that the gas turbine appears to be very efficient with 75 pst. efficiency and it is significantly higher than the average efficiency of other turbines on the Norwegian base. Oil protection is also an important part of this debate. Good environmental management in general is of great importance in relation to oil activities, both in order to be accepted and in order to have a minimum risk of accidents. This applies, as I mentioned in the introduction, to emissions at sea, where we have a requirement for zero emissions in normal operation. Boreq and boreq sludge must be reinjected or taken for treatment in line with the requirements of the Barents Sea in general. However, oil protection is very important and must be of high priority. It's had it from the builder, and it's had it from the committee, and it's had it from the government. It is important to have a robust, efficient and well-adapted oil protection system at local level. The oil companies, by the operator, are responsible for dealing with acute incidents from their own activities and for the dimension of their preparedness. In relation to the distance from land, it is particularly important to have a short period of emergency so that there is no undue risk of getting oil on land by accident. It is also worth mentioning that the design of oil protection is to be addressed by the State pollution control authority when the operator applies for authorisation for activities under the pollution law for production drilling and operation in 2011 and 2013 respectively, so that industry also has some years to develop further the existing oil protection technology. I would also like to say that it is positive, as a result of the Goliath project, that oil protection will generally be strengthened, when you know that shipping along the coast will increase in the future. As a case-by-case rapporteur, I am very pleased that the Committee has been as united as it has in many areas, and I am pleased that the first oil field in the Barents Sea can be started.

In the dialogue with the authorities, local politicians and industry, measures have been put in place to enhance the oil and gas competence of the Nordic economy, to promote the competitiveness of the Nordic industry, and to increase the interest in the energy sector among young people in the national divide. The lifetime of Goliat is estimated to be 15 years. It gives us time to build up competence and business in the region based on this thinking. The majority of the Committee agrees that exploration and field development in the region is necessary to maintain and increase the petroleum-related activity in the north. It is also necessary to increase the ring-effects. Goliath is located about 50 km at sea and partly within an area identified as particularly valuable and vulnerable. The development and operation of the field will therefore be subject to strict environmental requirements as laid down in the Barents Sea Management Plan. This means, among other things: the field shall meet the objective of zero emissions to the sea during normal operation. In addition, oil protection will reduce the impact of any emergency release. The oil protection system shall be dealt with at a later stage by the State pollution control authority. This will happen when the operator applies for authorisation for operations in 2011 and 2013 respectively. It has been very important for the Government to give high priority to oil protection, especially in the light of specific challenges in the north and the proximity of the field to the coast. We have therefore requested the builder to implement measures to strengthen oil protection in affected municipalities such as Måsøy, Hasvik and Nordkap. With this, we underline the importance of oil protection without prejudging the treatment of SFT. Finally, I am pleased that the members of the Committee support the Government's power-related assessment. I believe that we have reached a balanced solution by the fact that the power of Goliath comes partly from land and partly from the gas turbine on the device. I think it would be desirable for Goliath to be supplied exclusively with power from land. Unfortunately, it is unrealistic when the power situation in the region is as vulnerable as it is. In the long term, however, my ambition is full electrification of the field. It has therefore been important for me to start processes that can enable it. The government has agreed that Goliath will report the power consumption related to full electrification by the end of this year. We are making use of the new changes in the Energy Act to get a good process between the different key actors. We have also made provision for rightholders to submit a plan for increased use of force from land to Goliath as soon as the power situation in the area is strengthened, but no later than 1 January 2019. It is planned that the central network in the Hammerfest area will be strengthened in 2017, subject to the project being subject to a concession treatment under the Energy Act. In view of the situation in the region and the conditions we have set, I therefore believe that the power solution for Goliath per. Today is the best possible solution. Goliath will become an important force for industry and employment in the north. The field meets strict environmental requirements for petroleum activities and has a good and balanced solution. Goliath will therefore be one of our most important new building blocks in the north.

The exposure of the country shall be channelled primarily through those on the board of directors on behalf of the country. In addition, the State has annual contact meetings with the management of this company. One shall also bear in mind that the company is subject to the Share Act, with the obligations which it has just given rise to. This law applies to all shareholders, including the state. If one first starts by making exceptions to our state governance principle every time we think there are so-called special good reasons for it, I believe that we are on a dangerous path that it is difficult to get away from. The state must be a good and predictable owner, that's the only way to secure and further develop the great values that lie in our state-owned company in a longer perspective. That's the least we owe the Norwegian people when we manage these great values on behalf of all of us. There we read the following: Each barrel of tar oil, the bitumen, produces three times as much climate emissions as a barrel of conventional oil. Tar sand is the world's most water-intensive oil product. Each barrel requires consumption of three barrels of fresh water from the Athabasca River. The tar sand industry uses as much water every year as a city with two million inhabitants. To extract the tar oil, the industry burns enough fossil gas to warm up four million homes. The Alberta tar sand project will destroy or industrialise a Florida size forest, destroy biodiversity and the hydrology of the region forever. A report from WWF in the United Kingdom last year concluded that the exploitable potential for oil from tar sands and oil shale in North America will generate emissions of 980 billion tonnes of CO2. It is unfortunate, but understandable that oil and gas companies continue the activity they have historical competence in, but it is quite different that companies are now making a massive effort to develop new technologies to extract energy resources such as oil sands and oil shales, which the earth cannot bear to use." According to the fund, the tar sand project will lock us to the fossil age for 100 years in overtime. CO2 management is excluded because it is technically impossible. In addition, the tar oil will mainly be used to supply the US transport market, which is now in the midst of an important period of change. You have to go from petrol-extinguishing cars to energy-efficient cars, partly or completely powered by electricity. Perhaps the oil sand project will delay such a necessary transition. That would be bad in that case, because road traffic emissions cause a large part of the climate problem most of us, after all, worry about, with the exception of Ketil Solvik-Olsen and some of his like. The World Natural Fund also claims that this project has led to the largest natural intervention that Norway has ever been part of indirectly through StatoilHydro. We're talking about degradation or destruction of an area similar to South Norway. If this had been in the Amazon, we would have spent billions preserving the area, claims the Nature Fund. We are talking about wilderness, about untouched nature worth taking care of. The problem is that Canada itself is not of that opinion. ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e4350	The starting point for this management plan is the need to facilitate the sustainable development of key industries such as fisheries, petroleum, shipping, travel and energy activities, while at the same time ensuring the safety of the natural values of the marine environment. It's in many ways the long lines of the administration that are drawn up in the plan. The plan shall be dynamic and regularly updated, no later than 2014, and complete revision shall be carried out in 2025. The starting point is very good. The state of the environment in the Norwegian Sea is good. Yes, it is that the Norwegian Sea is one of the cleanest sea areas in the world and relatively unaffected by direct influence. It has been and is a broad support for the management plan. The endpoints are between the petroleum interests on the one hand and the environmental interests on the other. When these extremes in the view of the plan go a little bit in their separate direction, it may be a good basis to establish that the plan is balanced. With regard to the committee's examination of the management plan, I am delighted to be surprised at the broad agreement that the plan actually receives. With the background of the last two cases the Stortinget has now debated, one would almost not believe it was possible, but so is the case in this case. Similarly, a Joint Committee has adhered to the principle of comprehensive knowledge and ecosystem-based management. There is reason to say that in this area we once again confirm that we have moved the policy one step in the right direction. It is the knowledge base and sensible management principles that govern, not ideology. Against this background, there is reason to commend the Government for the work outlined in the conclusions and leads of the Storing Message. What, on the other hand, can give rise to concern is the conditions that we ourselves do not have direct control of. Like all other sea areas, the Norwegian Sea is affected by long-distance pollution. One example is marine acidification, with potential adverse effects already expected in 2025. I think a lot of attention needs to be paid to this issue in the coming years. The Norwegian Sea is the source and basis of a significant share of the total Norwegian value added. A Joint Committee is very concerned that this plan should, within an environmentally safe framework, facilitate increased value creation and production over the years to come. Should I personally allow myself to characterise the plan in one area that is not mentioned, it is my opinion that the plan underestimates something of the value and employment factor that lies in the opportunities available to the Norwegian Sea. In the Norwegian Sea, we find the largest fish stocks in the North-East Atlantic, which we share with other countries. In this area, we find spawning and breeding areas for several of the most important species. Therefore, Norway has a special responsibility to secure the areas against influences that could damage these resources in the future. The management of living marine resources is based on sustainable harvesting. This administrative principle is underlined by the Marine Resource Act which entered into force on 1 January of this year. An example of good administration is the reconstruction of Norwegian breeding herring. The main spawning areas are located outside Mora. This relationship is taken into account by the management plan and underlined in the setting. Fishermen have more than a thousand years of tradition of harvesting the sea just outside the front door. Today 14,500 annual works are directly employed in the fisheries and aquaculture industries of the four counties bordering on the management plan area. Now we are responsible for having an administration that allows this tradition to be carried on for thousands of years. Petroleum activity is the largest industry in the world. In the Norwegian Sea there has been petroleum activity since 1993, when the Draugen had its start-up. For 40 years Norway has been an oil and energy nation with significant development, much thanks to this industry. The development of a number of industries can be related to this activity. Very much of the technology and knowledge development in Norway comes as a consequence of this. It has become a Norwegian advantage that it has been and is subject to strict requirements for the petroleum activity. Restrictions and limitations are demanding, but it has given us a world-class industry in terms of knowledge, technology solutions and environmental standards. A good example of knowledge and technology development can be linked to the Kristin field. If we go back a few years, it was quite unimaginable that the Kristin field could be expanded and put into operation. The depth, pressure, temperature and formations should say that the field was not developed. The challenges were solved, and today it is such that they pick up gas and condensate every day from almost 5,000 meters deep. The pressure and temperature of the reservoir are 900 bar and 170 °C respectively. Yes, it is higher than in any field that has so far been built on a Norwegian base. This is a good example of how to solve the challenges of the future or today’s problems. It is therefore important that petroleum activities are provided with opportunities for development by making available new interesting exploration areas when the scope of knowledge and technology so requires. There are two reasons for this: the driving of knowledge and technology in the industry and the total value creation represented by the oil and gas industry. If we are to highlight and further develop the Norwegian welfare society, position ourselves for a carbon-neutral future and also be a helping hand for those people who really need assistance around the world, it will be a prerequisite that we facilitate such development. There have been relatively few conflicts between fisheries and petroleum activity during the 40 years of petroleum activity in Norwegian sea areas. In recent years, expanded seismic surveys have required more and more space to meet fishermen’s needs. Although seismic investigations are limited in time, conflicts have arisen. There is therefore reason to be satisfied that the Government has followed up this contradiction of interest, and that new rules are coming from 1 July this year. But I stress that it must be worked continuously to mitigate the contradictions of interest. Conflict potential must be reduced. As I said, the Norwegian Sea has unique natural values. Some areas are particularly important in order to maintain their integrity in the future. The coastal zone is of great importance to a number of activities and a lot of life. The bog banks, the Haltenbank and the Sklinnabank are important spawning areas. Sularevet and Iverback have their coral reefs. The edge of the egg is important with its versatile meaning. The Arctic front and Jan Mayen have significant environmental values in the form of large seabird colonies, large occurrences of plankton, fish and marine mammals. If these areas are to be addressed with due respect, there should have been a longer talk. I'm not going to, but I'm just going to stress the great importance of the areas. We know that it is discussed to open up activity in relation to, for example: Jan Mayen. But at the same time, it's the belief and confidence that we can move on with activities of different kinds and yet maintain the natural qualities that lead us on. The important thing now is the fact that the management plan is based on a balanced whole, with different industries and environmental considerations focused on each other – that we are taking steps at the right pace, that the steps are based on comprehensive knowledge of impacts, and that the very foundation, the precautionary principle, is maintained. I must pay tribute to the Government so that the management plan takes account of these considerations in a very good way. Finally, I would like to point out three very important areas for the future: The knowledge of MAREANO and SEAPOP must be of great importance in the further work on the management plan and decisions relating to updates and revisions. Oil protection is the second area. Oil protection equipment and monitoring must be continuously improved in order to reduce the likelihood of danger arising from increased ship traffic and petroleum activity. In the suspension, we assume that this happens during the management plan period. It is also important that the Government follow up on what the setting points to, and take an initiative to enable companies and technical environments together with the State to set up technology development programmes to improve oil protection. Emissions are also much discussed. It is important that good assessments are carried out for new and old fields which include overall environmental assessments of measures to prevent unwanted emissions, as well as cost-benefit assessments of setting zero emission requirements for new fields in the future. Finally, the Government has once again presented a good management plan for a large sea area in this big-scale communication. The consideration of the Committee is essentially coherent and underlines the importance of comprehensive and knowledge-based management. As you can see, a good management plan is being adopted during the night. And to put the case rapporteur's hat away for a final moment, there's reason to make the following clear: The government and government parties themselves are glued or centerpieces of a holistic and ecosystem-based maritime administration, and at the same time they are raising the industry's potential for development. Because it is a fact that even with the broad agreement that lies in the matter, the opposition is once again divided into important issues raised by the management plan.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e4463	Let me start by thanking the case rapporteur, Terje Aasland, and the Government, too, for the work done on this management plan for the Norwegian Sea. With this management plan, we've been tracking ourselves. We have established a new marine environment policy through the practice of management plans for marine areas. We started with a comprehensive management plan for the Barents Sea and the Lofoten in 2006 and now in 2009 with the Norwegian Sea, and then we will continue with the North Sea. In that case, that would mean 90 pst. of the Norwegian sea areas are covered. The management plan for the Barents Sea and Lofoten was the first management plan for a Norwegian sea area and thus a breakthrough. Work on management plans has also attracted international attention and is something we should be proud of. As we know, the Norwegian Sea has a rich nature, including large and internationally important populations of fish and seabird, and cold-water coral reefs that are unique in a global context. The specific environmental values in the Norwegian Sea require a high level of protection and environmental standards for activity in the area. The challenges in the Norwegian Sea are many. These include, in particular, the effects of climate change, acidification, over-taxation of individual fish stocks, risk of acute contamination, decline of seabird populations and the need for the conservation of coral reefs. So there's a variety of challenges that we have to deal with. That is why we must also continue and strengthen the implementation of a holistic and ecosystem-based management. Ecosystem-based management means that the management of human activities is based on the framework set by the ecosystem for the maintenance of structure, functioning, production and natural diversity – nothing less! This obligates. All marine activities shall now be managed within a framework that ensures that the overall environmental impact does not exceed the conservation of ecosystems. It challenges us because we lack a lot of knowledge about the area. That is why we also have to act as leaders of goods. SV is particularly satisfied with the constraints imposed on coastal and particularly natural areas in the Norwegian Sea. The dark coast is especially important to us. We are also very pleased that the Sularevet and Iverback are proposed as areas for marine protection and are protected against bottom radiation, and that the parts of the Iverback that are open to petroleum activities have been restricted by the fact that no concessions are being issued until the update of the management plan. The same is true of Fron and Sularevet. SV is pleased that we should keep a click on knowledge acquisition, for example. via MARANO and SEAPOP. I mentioned marine protective plans. This is pioneering work in Norway, for which we have great expectations. We are pleased that marine acidification has received extra attention in the message, a theme that has never been affected in political documents such as this. Climate change is going to challenge us strongly in the future. As a coastal nation, we are largely dependent on and responsible for the life of our marine areas. If we don't manage resources in a sustainable way, we say by the branch we're sitting on. At the same time, we're also an oil nation. This means that we often meet at sea in the door, and it is going to be a hotter topic in the future, as we are getting a better understanding of what climate change means for fisheries, bird life and the sea. Expected climate change impacts include, inter alia: changes in sea temperature, ocean currents and sea level. Increased CO2 levels in the atmosphere also lead to increased CO2 levels and acidification of sea water. We do not know how fast and how climate change will affect the marine environment in the Norwegian Sea. However, heating of the Norwegian Sea is expected to lead to a shift of the fronts between Atlantic and Arctic waters northwards and westwards. The specific nature of the Norwegian Sea means that the impact of marine acidification is expected to be particularly rapid here, with adverse effects on ecosystems already by 2025. That is why we are now focusing on building up knowledge and monitoring. Since the beginning of the industrial revolution, water near the sea surface in the world's oceans has already been reduced by approximately pH. 0,1 pH units. Over the next 100 years, a decrease of 0,3 pH units is expected, depending on emission scenarios. This is a trend that is 100 times faster than for the previous 100,000 years. In case of increased intake of CO2 in sea water, acidification occurs which is expressed at reduced pH. As a result, the calcareous shelling organisms have reduced the ability to form calcareous shells. In addition, we have a scare scenario, and that's that because of too much CO2 absorption, the sea is starting to give up CO2 again. Then we get so-called feedbacks, which can really speed up climate change. We have to deal with this, and in the SV's view, all the knowledge we now have should ensure that we get a directed rotation from oil to new renewable energy sources.
ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e4497	The Norwegian Sea is an area far larger than the Norwegian mainland. It is therefore an important milestone when we finally get a comprehensive management plan for this area. The Norwegian Sea has a rich nature, including large and internationally important populations of fish and seabird as well as cold-water coral reefs that are unique in a global context. In the Marine Environment Communication, the Bondevik II Government laid the foundation for a whole new thinking on the management of our marine areas. In turn, they will have their own management plans, where for the first time there will be a holistic and ecosystem-based management of the Norwegian sea and coastal areas. This means that we should no longer be able to take decisions that expose a marine environment to environmental impact, or risk of environmental impact, without putting this into full use, and that the environmental impact or risk is assessed together. This is why it represents a milestone in environmental policy. Ecosystem-based management means that the management of human activities is based on the framework set by the ecosystem for the maintenance of its structure, functioning, production and natural diversity. The Christian People's Party believes that the management plan had been stronger if the Natural Diversity Act had not given it the limitations it had in relation to marine areas. Nevertheless, the Christian People's Party joins the main lines of the message. It is important that we have now also defined the particularly valuable areas in the Norwegian Sea, and that this is relevant to the decisions we make in these areas. Here the Government, according to the Christian People's Party's opinion, has not been able to show a whole and clear line. It is difficult from the Communication to look at clear environmental criteria that justify the Government's policy for each area. Whenever a reader of the message, a few impressions may be that petroleum activities in the TFO regions give rise to another form of environmental impact or give rise to less risk than other petroleum activities. It shows, in our view, that there is a need to assess more closely the basis and effects of the allocation scheme in predefined areas. Furthermore, the Government announces that it will initiate an opening process for petroleum activities in the Norwegian sea areas on the part of Jan Mayen for the purpose of granting concessions. We're still looking for the line of this government in these questions. In the message, the government shows transparency for oil drilling up to 30 km from Jan Mayen. The Barents Sea Management Plan establishes a limit of 50 km from land, after several years of investigations and environmental assessments. Jan Mayen is, among other things, nesting area for several major seabird species. It is difficult to see what assessments are behind this, and the communication at this point gives a signal that environmental concerns are on the wane front, and that in this government other considerations are gaining too often at the expense of the environment. We are pleased that we have arrived that we finally have a management plan for a large and resource-rich area. A holistic and ecosystem-based management imposes great demands on the practical follow-up, where the basic principles must not be overarched by short-term profits.
ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e4514	Let me start by commending the case manager for a thorough and good work, and also for a good presentation of the case here in Stortinget. As a coastal nation, we have at all times taken wealth out of our oceans – fish and marine mammals for more than a thousand years, and in the last decades also petroleum resources. In addition, our marine areas are areas of high transport activity, and in the future they can be used to produce large amounts of renewable energy. Our marine areas hide important natural areas such as cold water coral reefs and areas that are particularly important food and growing areas for different species of fish. Good and resilient management of our marine areas is therefore of great importance both in terms of nature, industry and the environment – now, and in the future. We are very satisfied with the fact that we have now established important management plans for two of our major marine areas, and are concerned that we will also have a follow-up as soon as possible when it comes to North Sea management. Co-existence between the different industries operating in our seas and beyond nature and the environment is sometimes quite demanding. We need good plans to regulate this coexistence. Extraction of petroleum is not problem-free either from the point of view of nature or the environment. Although the players on the Norwegian pedestal are among the world's leading environmental and technology players, we do not avoid the fact that activity through seismic activity, drilling activity and transport to and from has environmental and nature implications. The management plan shall contribute to the framework of this activity, in such a way that it is done in the most reasonable manner possible, and shall, however, determine the area to be and not to be opened for such activity. Requirements for zero emissions should be actively applied to new installations and should be assessed on a case-by-case basis when existing infrastructure is involved. This value has not been without risk. 2008 was a historic year in that way. 2008 was the first year of all times when all fish families along the coast experienced getting all their homes alive. 2009 is unfortunately more gloomy. But what is an even greater threat than the forces of nature at sea is we who manage it, if that administration is not resilient. We don't have the full overview of the consequences of petroleum activity, and we also know that there may be other activities in these areas that pose risks to the ecosystem. Then this is a point of departure for the precautionary principle to avoid being old with our large fish resources. There is an almost inexhaustible potential for offshore wind power in the Norwegian seas if this type of energy production becomes profitable. It is very positive that the government is in the forefront and is working on a law for windmills at sea. The distance of ship traffic from land can be recalculated in minutes and hours if there is a breakdown and/or oil spillage. Valuable response time to limit the damage of such events is important. The distance to land is also inherently rice-coding for maritime traffic. Therefore, it is gratifying that the Government will continue to lay the rent further off the coast even in the sea areas of the son of Røst. Knowledge must be at the heart of management, and it is therefore very important that the MAREANO and SEAPOP programmes be given great importance, and it is important that the Committee emphasises that they should also be further developed to include new areas such as: Jan Mayen area.

Response time through distance from countries is then crucial. Therefore, the Left believes that if the government parties are to continue the opening process around Jan Mayen, it must at least be set as a limit that it should be 50 km away from where the activity is started. It's been a few years since the Left suggested in Stortinget that a strategy be developed for marine energy production. Then it was voted down by the government parties. Then we got it into the climate agreement, and the government has announced that it would come to Stortinget before the summer. This has not happened, but there is at least reason to call for full pressure on this issue, because there is an area of enormous potential, but where there is also a need to balance different areas of conflict and to establish procedures for further work in this area.
ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e4569	Let me also thank the case rapporteur for both a good post and a good job, and let me join the many people who have said that this is a historic day. It is of great international importance that Norway first prepares a management plan for the Barents Sea, then for the Norwegian Sea, and will complete its work on a similar management plan for the North Sea. In all contacts with Nordic and other international colleagues, this is something that is valued, as noted, and from which other countries are interested in studying the experience. Since some members of the opposition have spent time hacking small, let me also add that there is a significant difference between what the Bondevik government did, namely to declare that it was a good idea to make such management plans, and the very laborious, difficult work to balance all the interests in the Barents Sea and in the Norwegian Sea, and to get into a management plan that is – as Tord Lien quite rightly said – a protective plan, but a balanced protection plan. It is a management plan which takes into account that the specific environmental values of the Norwegian Sea require a high level of protection and high environmental standards. Let me mention some of the most important new grips that are being done here, and that have a big, long-term environmental significance. Firstly, no new concessions for petroleum activities are announced at the Moravian Banks. It is a very clear signal that since these are as important as spawning grounds, you will not be able to start petroleum activities there. Secondly, the protection of coral reefs is strengthened. It is a new approach, which should perhaps have been taken earlier in Norwegian policy, but which is at least now being taken, and which is absolutely crucial to protecting the coral reefs. Thirdly, we are formally introducing marine protective plans. A number of areas will be considered for this purpose. It's a parallel to the protection work that's been driven to land for a long time. Now we're expanding it by also starting protective plans at sea. And perhaps most importantly, we are strengthening the level of knowledge about the effects of marine acidification. I can mention that internationally, it is more and more marked as one of the really great consequences of climate change that the seas are getting acidified. We are now far from having full knowledge of what this will mean, but there is every reason to take a serious view of the situation. Sea acidification will lead to major problems for a number of smaller animals, especially shellfish and lime-based animals, leading to major problems throughout the food chain. Many researchers talk about this as one of the really serious impacts of climate change. It is also one of the relatively few impacts of climate change that, in all likelihood, impact more strongly on Norway than on the international scene. In most areas, the effects of climate change are much tougher in tropical strata and in more vulnerable areas like China and India than they are up here in the rugged North, but marine acidification seems to be becoming a bigger problem in cold waters than in hot waters. That is why this will be a particular problem for Norwegian coastal areas. So there's every reason to take it seriously, and we start working on it through this management plan. The management plan contains a number of other good measures. Erling Sande and others have been involved in laying the ship's lease further out of the country. It is an important measure that increases the level of protection. Action is taken against uncontrolled and uncontrolled fishing. Of course, it is important and in line with what has been Norwegian policy for many years. And new measures are being taken to protect seabirds. All in all, there is a balanced plan, but there is an environmental plan and a protection plan that internationally puts us in the driver's seat in terms of taking care of sea areas. But far more important than being able to boast about it internationally is the fact that this is an incredibly important step in ensuring absolutely crucial natural qualities for future generations in this country. The plan is ecosystem-based and knowledge-based and of course needs to be rotated over the years as all the new knowledge we are going to get through the various research programmes, which are also part of the whole.

Most of the discussion has been about the decision to start the opening process in the area around Jan Mayen with a view to distributing concessions. It's an area that is believed to be vulnerable and that has unique environmental values that we per. today has limited knowledge of it. I would therefore like to point out that an overall committee is clear that a comprehensive survey of the area is necessary for the proper functioning of the opening process. Particular reference is made to the programmes for the monitoring of seabirds and seabeds, Seapop and MAREANO, and the importance of these programmes for the collection of knowledge in the area. There is no doubt that there are large protection values associated with the area, and there is a process in progress to protect Jan Mayen as a nature reserve. It is important that any petroleum activity is so remote from the nature reserve that the protection values are not impaired. There is an overall committee that is also behind that clarification. Then it's stated in the proposition that there should be no petroleum activity within a 30-kilometre belt around Jan Mayen. The area outside is to be investigated. This means that, following the impact assessment, it is determined how close countries it may be to activity. It must be based on professional assessments and the knowledge gained from the overall survey to be carried out. But in any case, petroleum activity within these 30 kilometers is not relevant. Then I also think it is important to say that it is Iceland's announced activity that is the triggering reason why the question of Jan Mayen has now become relevant. Norway and Iceland have a common administrative area, and Norwegian value creation has been crucial in choosing to start an opening process. There's an assessment that's specific to Jan Mayen and the common administration area we have there. It shall not be such that Jan Mayen automatically becomes an opening port into arctic strokes for the petroleum activity. All such possible future assessments must be made on the basis of sound and comprehensive knowledge and, of course, the precautionary principle should always be taken into account. I would also like to make a few brief comments on the issue of emissions to the sea. State pollution control, together with the State Radiation Protection Agency and the Oil Directorate, has not recommended the introduction of a general zero emission requirement, as has been done with regard to the management area of the Barents Sea, the Lofoten and the Western Sea. The report is not being consulted and the input to the consultation round shall be weighted. I would like to mention here in particular the Marine Research Institute, which has a great deal of professional weight on this issue. It is also worth noting that the Committee pays particular attention to the emission requirements to be applied in regions with vulnerable fauna, such as the environment. coral reefs and central spawning areas for bottom spawning fish. In such areas, the use of cap and drilling fluid management technologies shall be required to prevent sludge. It's important to be told. At the end of the day, I would like to draw up a case which has been under discussion around, but which deserves attention. It is that with this plan we are alerting the launch of a process for marine protection plans during 2009. Norway has committed itself internationally to the establishment of a network of marine protected areas. By protecting some of our most unique areas, we are following up on these commitments. It's basically smaller areas within 12 nautical miles, but they have very special ecological and natural qualities. It is primarily the type of areas that are currently being protected, and I am pleased that we are now getting a systematic effort to take care of these areas.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e4807	It is a key objective of Norwegian fisheries policy that we should contribute to long-term sustainable value creation and a resource use that is beneficial to the whole of society. Both settlement and nutrient development in Coast Norway have been and are dependent on fisheries. Therefore, we must manage the marine environment in such a way that we can continue to reap the resources of the sea as long as the oil age is over. In the Norwegian Sea there are spawning and growing areas for several of our main fish stocks. 90 pst. Of the populations that we harvest, we share with other countries. That's why it's so important that we see our own management of the area from an international perspective, and it underlines our special responsibility to protect these areas from harmful influences. The management of living marine resources is based on sustainable harvesting. This administrative principle has been maintained and underlined by the Marine Resource Act, which entered into force on 1 January of this year. The law requires increased research and monitoring efforts precisely because increased knowledge continues to contribute to improved resource management. The importance of research for coastal development is also illustrated by what is called bio-prospecting, and in particular the research on cold-resistant organisms. Here it can actually be that we can find the solution to the cancer problem, or we can find the active ingredients that make it possible to control HIV and AIDS. I believe that marine biotechnology will gradually open up completely new business opportunities, which of course we also need to take into account in our management plans. An ecosystem-based management means that human activity in the sea does not compromise nature’s resilience. The management plan must therefore be a tool for value creation and for maintaining environmental values in marine areas. It is clearly clear that fisheries have an impact on ecosystems. We have overfished some of our fish stocks, partly because of insensibility, partly because of the desire for financial gain. It's not always as easy to rebuild populations once the damage is done, but it's possible if we want to do it. An example of this, also referred to by the case-rapporteur, is the population of Norwegian springing herring, which lay down for many years. We actually stopped fishing for such herring. This year the marine scientists have recommended that we take out a total of 1.6 million tonnes of Norwegian herring herring. Silda slips on the bottom, and the fertilized eggs are attached to the bottom. When the eggs hatch, the larvae rise to the surface water, where they passively drift northwards with the currents of the sea. The point is that the areas for such gything are pretty limited, and that's why we've protected important spawning grounds off the Mure coast. I'm willing to say that I think it's a strength for the whole plan that we've protected these areas. I think we're going to recognise that our economic activity in the marine areas sometimes creates conflicts, and that is particularly true between oil and fish, but eventually also with regard to increasing ship traffic and the establishment of offshore wind turbines. Of course, we need to do our part to ensure that these conflicts of interest can be resolved as much as possible, and co-existence between different industries is also a very important objective in the management plan. So far, there have been few conflicts between oil and fish, but as the oil industry has moved northwards, seismic surveys in vulnerable areas have provoked the anger of fishermen. In particular, this is about seismic shooting outside of Lofoten and the West Sea last year, and I must say that the Oil Directorate could have shown a better understanding of the implementation of the last year's programme. This has led the fisheries and oil authorities to re-examine the legislation on this type of activity and to propose a number of changes. Then we'll see if this helps to mitigate the conflicts.
