ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e5451	The question of national cod fjords has long been discussed as an instrument for limiting the damage of cod farming to the environment and not least to local cod stocks. New knowledge shows that the coastal cod is not a homogeneous group, but that, along our long-range coast, unique genetic strains of coastal cod are confined to a geographical area. Several of these unique genetic strains are disappearing, and we are losing hugely important resources that are particularly important to the coastal fleet. Cod farming is a relatively new industry in Norway, but it has grown at record speed in recent years. Since 2003, production has increased by 20–25 pst. each year, and production is expected to reach 30 000 tonnes in 2010. The environmental impact of cod farming still has more questions than answers. What we know is that 80 pst. of all cattle for rearing slaughtered have guttered at least once. At the Ostenvoll Research Station of the Institute of Marine Research, they have been confirmed that the breeding seal survives and evolves, but whether the breeding seal works positively or negatively on the wild cod populations remains an unanswered question. Breeding storks have also been xenophobic, as the migration statistics of the Directorate of Fisheries show. In 2007, 69000 breeding marshes escaped. In 2008, the number increased to 183 000. The Left is pleased that the Government is to assess whether cod fjords should be introduced and that other investigations are to be carried out with a view to increasing knowledge of possible environmental risk factors related to cod farming. I have noticed that in some areas the government has started the investigation work on introducing national cod fjords as a management tool, and that the Ministry of Fisheries and Coastal Affairs will order new investigations. The left is aware that this investigation will take time and believes that until the knowledge is acquired, a restrictive distribution of new cod farming concessions must be carried out. In ‘New building blocks in the north. The next step in the Northern Region Strategy of the Government is to facilitate a national initiative on cod farming in the northern regions. The left shares the government's future-optimism for the breeding of cod, but asks questions at the pace in which the industry grows. For the Left, it is very important that a sustainable and profitable development of the industry is put in place. Today, those who earn money in cattle cattle are exempt, as the reality for most is that the cost of production is much higher than the price in the market. Therefore, in the view of the Left, it is very important to intensify the work to bring more knowledge to and about the industry. I am also of the opinion that, by pursuing a restrictive allocation and growth policy for cod farming until the environmental impact is known, efforts are being made to ensure that economic development can take place within a cost-effective and sustainable framework. The left considers that in the light of the knowledge acquired on cod farming, and the recommendations made by the Institute of Marine Research, the Directorate of Fisheries and the Regulatory Committee, we should introduce national cod fjords as a management tool. The left considers that this solution does not need to be permanent, as new technology and knowledge can lead to the non-recoverable damage of cod farming to the environment by the disappearance of unique strains of genetic cod. In its Northern Region Strategy, the Government has facilitated a national effort to breed cod in the northern regions. While the Government will invest in cod farming, the Government is tightening up by granting access restrictions, cf. Ot. pp. No 77 for 2008-2009, About the Law amending the Aquaculture Act, and has now opened up to introduce cod-free areas. For me, the government speaks with two tongues here. The left questions how the Government intends to implement a national initiative on cod farming, while at the same time giving access restrictions and entering in order to protect areas for cod farming. The left side is pleased that the Government opens up to protect coastal cod areas and believes that this will ensure the industry's long-term and sustainable framework, but those to invest in the industry need predictable frameworks. I cannot understand that this kind of double communication is good for the further development of the industry. I ask the Council of State to say something about how the Government will put up its large-scale investment in cod farming. I do not promote a proposal of my own, but as one sees the matter, we will support the proposal made by the Christian People's Party and the Right in the vote. Sigvald Uppøen Hansen had here taken over the president's seat.
ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e5468	The President will correct it – it is a proposal from the Advance Party and the Right.
ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e5471	I'm sorry!
ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e5476	The government believes that cod farming will be an important part of coastal value creation. Whitefish industry has had a heavy winter, but in the long term cod in farming could become an important complement to wild cod and enable European consumers to offer high-quality fresh cod throughout the year. Environmentally sustainable operations are essential for long-term growth and industry profitability. The fisheries management strategy for cod farming is knowledge-based, and in recent years we have invested a lot in research in this area. However, further development requires more knowledge of how cod farming affects the environment and wild fish. The government will contribute to controlled growth, in parallel with the development of technologies and knowledge that ensure a sustainable and forward-looking industry. The proposal for a representative proposes that the Government carry out a professional investigation into cod-free zones in order to provide knowledge of the need to introduce national cod fjords as an appropriate management tool. We think this is a good proposal, and therefore the Government already decided in February this year that such an investigation should be carried out. This was first launched by the Minister of Fisheries and Coast in Bergen on 12 February this year and is subsequently integrated into the Government’s strategy for an environmentally sustainable aquaculture industry. The introduction of national cod fjords, or cod protection areas, could be an instrument for protecting local and special strains of coastal cod. We have already introduced territorial protection that restricts farming in the past, to the protection of the wild species, through the introduction of national salmon fjords and salmon waters. The first round was promoted by the Bondevik II government, and the second round was promoted by the sitting government. Both proposals were adopted with broad agreement in Stortinget. The forthcoming study on cod fjords will highlight both the positive and the negative aspects of such a scheme and provide a basis for assessing the appropriate management action. In addition to the issue of cod fjords, we will give priority to research aimed at increasing knowledge of environmental risk factors related to cod farming, both in terms of impacts on ecosystems and on wild cod populations. This includes, inter alia: the mapping of spawning grounds and the monitoring of particularly vulnerable populations of coastal cod. It'll take time for us to put in place all the knowledge we want. Several of the investigations are large and complex and are likely to take three to five years to complete, but the administration will, of course, make use of the knowledge as it is generated. In the short term, we are in the process of tightening up the legislation so that the authorities can control the growth of the cod farming industry, among others. through amendments to the Aquaculture Act adopted at Laginget on 16 June of this year. Cod farming is still a small industry. Production in 2008 was below 15 000 tonnes. In comparison, we produced 820 000 tonnes of salmon. It will still take many years before the cod farming industry is a major industry, and the grips we are taking now do not intend to prevent the industry from growing and developing in the coming years. The work we do at an early stage in providing us with broad and good knowledge of how the industry affects wild fish and the surrounding environment will be crucial in order to be able to operate knowledge-based management in the future.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e5600	Agriculture is an important industry for both the Norwegian people and the Stortinget. The right is behind the central objectives of agricultural policy. But before I come to the Right's many amendments, I would like to say a little pleased that a majority, consisting of all parties other than the Progress Party, believes that 'agriculture policy must be gradually modernised on the basis of today's arrangements with, among other things, market organisations, geographical differentiation in quota and grant schemes and the use of the customs protection area to enable farmers and the food industry to adapt to developments'. In my opinion, this was perhaps one of the better performances of the socialist side of agricultural policy. Unfortunately, in Norway the sheep industry has been left-handed in politics for many years. The government and agricultural organisations have not had visible ambitions to develop this part of the agricultural industry. The production of lamb is on the waving front, and the industry is extremely small-scale. The problem is that the sheep industry has been a kind of alibi for the policy objective of varying usage structures. The grant scheme has expired, and it is characterised by an excessively strong structure profile. This means that the premium per sheep falls heavily the more animals you have, a device that prevents progress towards greater and more modern use. So while milk producers have organized themselves for the longest time in larger units and cooperatives, little of this has happened in the sheep industry. But there are some very interesting exceptions. For example, Vegard Birkeland in Sauda joined three other sheep farmers in Sauda and built a new, very sheepy farm last year of 1600 m2. It is planned that this poultry shall have between 500 and 600 winter-fed sheep at a time. I think the Minister of Agriculture and the Ministry of Agriculture should take a study trip to Sauda and take a closer look at modern sheep fever. In Valle municipality in the Setes Valley, the number of winter-fed sheep has halved in the last ten years. The effect is re-growth and reduced production. There are some sheep farmers who want to join together for a large co-operative poultry, also referred to as a sheep hotel. The problem is that prospective sheep farmers both in Valle and Sauda are being countered by the red-green government. The premium scheme stops short and well at 300 animals, leaving one of the most premium intensive productions without any subsidies when passing a medium-sized holding. Therefore, on 20 March this year, the right asked the Minister of Agriculture whether it was not time to change the arrangement and to facilitate the size of sheep as well. The answer was as usual that the Government invested in the transfer of more subsidies, but there was otherwise no sign of innovation and modernisation. The sheep industry also needs a lighthouse to show the way, which shows that it is possible to invest and invest on this part of the farm on a slightly larger scale. The right, therefore, promotes in the vote a proposal where we ask the Government to facilitate co-operation and greater use of sheep. Unfortunately, it is only the Progress Party that is in favour of this proposal. I find it sad that Norwegian farmers are being made tenants of today's property regulation. It is a paradox that owners of agricultural property should not have ownership rights in line with other owner-occupiers and owners in that country. Housing obligations, bans on sharing and price regulation are an old socialist system that impedes the development of the structure of agriculture and weakens the values of the farmer. Deteriorated ownership obviously results in a decrease in investment intentions and a corresponding decrease in development. The effect of property law is becoming very visible in Norway. There are 35,000 empty courtyards around. There are less than 50 000 active farmers, although there are almost 200,000 agricultural holdings. The rent fraction has probably passed 40 pst., without this triggering any signs of reform in the red-green parts. Even when research indicates that housing is useless as a district policy instrument also in the field of agricultural property, some react. It was almost to be thought that the legislation was more important than both the farmer and agriculture. Right will have a marked strengthening of the farmer's ownership, and we'll make suggestions about it in the setting. With regard to the agricultural settlement, the members of the Committee from the right shall make a change in relation to previous settlements during that period. Although we do not agree to this year's settlement, we do not propose replacing the entire settlement with a consultation arrangement. Nevertheless, the Right continues to consider that it is in principle a concern that individual industries negotiate their own framework conditions directly with the State and conclude agreements with significant financial implications outside the budgetary procedure. There are also other aspects of the agricultural policy and the negotiating institute that need to be considered. First, grants need to be more targeted and less bureaucratic than they are today. Therefore, the right considers that the periodicisation of agricultural production should be modified to follow the budgetary year. This will allow negotiations to take place in parallel with the budgetary process and to be seen in conjunction with other priorities of the State budget. To this end, the right is in favour of proposing that the agricultural policy should be shifted and should be part of the budgetary procedure for 2010. An overall opposition promotes many proposals that show alternative political responses to the government’s agricultural policy approach. The party of progress, the Right, the Christian People's Party and the Left stand, for example. proposals for the investigation of tax reliefs for farmers, for the decoration of co-operative legislation and for the development of the potential of niche and specialty products. The Progress Party, the Right and the Left are united on a number of other proposals that show the way in agricultural policy. There are still considerable differences now if we are to take seriously the proposals of the Progress Party, but there are still many good input from the minority and opposition in the vote, to which the government parties unfortunately are not. There is therefore every reason to conclude that Norwegian farmers will have good framework conditions and new interesting opportunities for development as entrepreneurs if developments become the same as the case rapporteur had said, that we will have a non-socialist majority on the Stortinget for the autumn. I would like to address the suggestions in this setting and in the setting of the reindeer agreement, which the Right has with others or alone.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e5884	I will present this year's reindeer management agreement, which is a good deal for the reindeer owners, with a framework of 101 million. NOK, which is an increase of 4 million. kr in relation to the last year’s agreement. I thank the Committee for working together on this setting, which I experience has followed the same pattern as before, and so far has been a good job. But reindeer is still facing major challenges. There are still considerable differences in economic performance in the industry between the various grazing areas. The compensation for work and equity amounts to NOK 356 000 per year for South-Trendelag and Hedmark. annual works, while the equivalent for areas in West Finland is down to NOK 92 000. This says something about the need to change the operational arrangements. There is too much reindeer in some areas and terriblely low slaughter weights, and it may seem incomprehensible that the reindeer in these areas themselves are unable to grasp it. This year's agreement is the second after we got a new reindeer management law, a law that dramatically changed the internal organisation of reindeer operations, made sure that reindeer owners are held responsible for the balance of the reindeer with the resource base, and clarified the responsibility of the state to intervene when this is not properly respected. The process of changing the organisation and management of reindeer operations is under way. The development of usage rules and the setting of reindeer numbers is a priority task. Both the process of establishing criteria for an ecologically sustainable clean-up, and in particular the fact that management, in cooperation with the industry, was aimed at this work, are new in the management of reindeer management. One has developed a good dialogue with industry and a common understanding of the challenges facing one. The deadline for the districts to submit implementing rules is set at 1 July 2009. It's in this area that I believe the opposition is missing in its criticism of the Government. This is a process to be anchored, which must take some time. Here's a lot to change. This has to do with traditions and culture, but the government has prioritised and put in place the law and follows up the work on internal organisation, rules of use and a top reindeer with firmness and authority. We'll eventually get a reindeer that has ecological, economic and cultural sustainability. It has a high priority. In my view, the proposal for a big message in the middle of this falls on its own unfairness. Now it's working to make the new regime work that needs priority. Then, in my view, it is relevant to raise the question of an evaluation of the work that has been done, and in that context, the question of a new monitoring report on reindeer management can be a relevant issue. It's not the time of the majority and my view now. I recommend that the setting be adopted.
ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e5905	I see in the proposition that, in addition to some changes, the reindeer management agreement with production-dependent grants from 2003 to 2004 has been continued. In addition, the Government has introduced a specific scheme for West Finland, a grant to replace the calf-slaughter and early slaughter premium, in order to try to reduce the number of animals. It's such a big bet that it goes over a whole year. It specifies the objectives of the reindeer management policy and chooses to call it a sustainable reindeer management based on ecological sustainability, economic sustainability and cultural sustainability. It is easy to surround yourself with great, fine words, but is a glorifying word like ‘sustainability’ according to reality? Then I would like to point out that I know that there are major local and regional differences in how the practice of reindeer operations actually takes place. Ecological sustainability for reindeer management means that it is largely related to what nature is able to produce from grazing resource base. In western Finland, over 30,000 animals have been lost for a long time, with the result that the grazing resource base disappears. The western dimension is at risk of becoming a high mountain desert, while the government claims that ecological sustainability should be the starting point. I don't know where the government will, but using words like "organic sustainability" while the government doesn't have the ability to clean up in a situation where the entire ecology is at risk of breaking down on the western horizon, is tragic – both for ecology and for industry. Economic sustainability shall be another supporting element. Economic sustainability means that an industry is able to stand on its own feet and operate economically. Is this the case in the reindeer industry? The industry as such has an annual gross turnover of approx. 320 million. kr. Half of this, or about 160 million. kr, are values in the form of meat price, processing, etc. The rest are state subsidies that are driving, and increasing the number of animals, this makes the government disappear at the same time as the whole of ecology disappears, and the last bit is compensation payments for the loss of animals. Weak Simlars get too weak calves, which in turn have little chances of growing up, and replacements increase. This is called economic sustainability. I almost have to ask, is it so that the Storting should take its role seriously, or are we going to relate to approximate parodies? It might be interesting if we were dealing with the reality and the real problems that the industry has, and starting from the areas where the industry labors the hardest. In an area with an excessive number of animals, it is ecological and economic madness to continue systems that directly cause more animals to grow. This kind of policy will end up with the fact that no one can live from reindeer drift on the west in a reasonably near future. Today’s regime and policies are helping to increase the problems in industry rather than solve them. As far as the western dimension is concerned, you should convert to more stationary reindeer rather than the nomadic that we have today. Look at what Sweden and Finland have done, and look at the slaughter weight there, it's not going to be much to understand that reforming today's regime is absolutely necessary. The question then arises: will the industry and those who have converted to make money on today's failed subsidised schemes be entitled to cope with such a conversion? I guess the answer is the same as it would be in the fisheries. If each fisherman is to determine his own quotas, we are to clear the sea. The same is true of the reindeer industry, not so as to understand that the sea is being emptied, but the west could be empty of animals and become a wasteland if we do not change the policy in these areas.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-18.ana.ud66e5940	In 1974, State Council Treholt returned from the UN meeting in Rome under the auspices of the FAO and said that Norway had to strengthen agriculture, shift the diet to more cereal products instead of meat in order to take the world’s difficult food situation seriously. We got St.meld. No 32, Nutritional Notice, 1975, Hitra Action in August 1975, St.Gröttingen Stepping Decision 1 December 1975 and the legendary St.meld. No 14 from 1976, cultivated more land – strengthening Norwegian self-sufficiency. We got a green wave, youth got a hope for the future in work on soil, agriculture – using soil, gaining solar energy, photosynthesis, green fingers and caring for animals. Today, the most important thing is similar to 1974: the international food crisis, about 1 billion people starve, the Irish presented their "Hunger Task Force Report" to the UN last autumn. But much is also very different from the 1970s: strong population growth, hundreds of millions more mouths to satisfy, warmer climates to give great crop variations, far more widespread spread of viruses and infection on plants, animals and humans, much more population living in urban areas, less knowledge among people most about the production and processing of food. Therefore, the situation is much more serious now than it was in 1974. Participation in the UN Conference confirms this. In 1986 we received the mandate of the so-called Uruguay Round in GATT. The point was new international trade rules for food that would result in lower production and away with a dumped market at prices below sea level. However, no one believed that the reality of the Uruguay Round and established legislation would continue to be practised in 2009-23 years later in a food shortage. The WTO must have a new agricultural mandate. Minister of Foreign Affairs Støre has traveled a great deal and has seen that national leaders want to feed their own people as much as possible. In our country, however, people can travel to the store every day and buy what they need for the next day. So most people don't feel vulnerability. The red-green government has made a positive difference to the former right-wing government. But what about the hope of a good life, with farm work for the biologically interested youth? The hope of social respect through cohesive economic conditions, as we got in the 1970s, is not yet lit. A new national policy is needed to take seriously the international food crisis. The framework conditions for agriculture must be strengthened on a broad front. We need to strengthen import protection, build Norwegian agriculture on plough chills, not ship purchases, assume that Norway is a small country of agriculture, have a profitable grazing where predators play second fiolin, reduce the market power of food chains for the benefit of the food industry and farmers. At all: we're in a crisis. This gives hope, because the established truths have to be reconsidered. The centre party must be in the front to chisel out the new future course. No parties in this room have a greater responsibility to succeed in giving young people hope for the future. Family ownership and love for soil must be the basis for the new policy.
